Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

GitHub’s new Git storage architecture is built so thousands of agents can push to a single repository at once. Here’s how it works.

Last week at GitHub Universe, we announced our new Git storage architecture, capable of handling thousands of writes per second with reads that scale on demand. Through internal testing, we’ve demonstrated the ability to support 5,000 concurrent agents working on a repository, sending 7,200 pushes per second to a repo, all while serving read traffic with consistently low latency. This new architecture is the foundation for GitHub to support agentic development for hundreds of millions of developers, and we’ve been building it underneath GitHub while it keeps running.

Over the past year, agents changed what a busy repository looks like on GitHub. In our first post in this series, we laid out the two principles behind our next-generation Git storage architecture: minimize coordination, and decouple storage from compute. In our last post, we covered the bridge: moving 99.99999% of repositories into Azure, scoping the write quorum to individual references, and standing up virtual replicas backed by Azure Blob Storage.

We also left off with an observation: very little of a write needs to be ordered. Git objects are immutable and named by their contents, meaning two writers can store them at the same time without agreeing on anything. The part of a push that needs agreement is the reference update, and a reference update is tiny in comparison. Virtual replicas were the first step toward building around that, and the means by which we onboard repositories to the new architecture.

The rest of the architecture builds on that observation. Only reference updates need agreement. That lets the parts of Git infrastructure that used to be tightly coupled (storing data, serving requests, and maintaining repositories) scale independently. For developers, Git stays Git: they push, fetch, and branch the same way, with the same protections in place. Below, we walk through the requirements behind the design, follow a request through the system, and dive deep into two of its components.

We think about the scale of the GitHub platform along two axes:

Today, the busiest repositories on GitHub.com receive at most dozens of writes in a single second, typically in short bursts. As more of that work shifts to agents running in parallel and around the clock, we expect those bursts to become a sustained rate within the next year. Each push leads to more reads: CI runs, deployment systems pull the new commit, and other agents fetch it for context. This read fan-out results in a large amplification effect on overall traffic that scales with writes.

We’re already seeing this trend play out on repositories at the top of the curve. The chart below shows daily requests to one repository on GitHub from March to May, when its traffic grew about 4x. Its growth has continued on an exponential trajectory since then.

We have to plan for a world where agent-driven development keeps accelerating at this speed. Instead of planning for the next increment, we set a target of at least two orders of magnitude more scale. For a single repository, that meant the new architecture needed to:

On top of that, the system had to be able to improve overall reliability and scale globally.

In May of 2026, hot on the heels of the initial Azure migration, we started working on a design that could meet those demands. We tried several approaches. In all of them, pack data lived in Azure Blob Storage, and in some we experimented with keeping reference state there too. We’ll cover some of the trade-offs we found below, but we settled on a design we felt maximized throughput while preserving the correctness, durability, availability, and latency guarantees a platform like GitHub requires. We called it “Rubicon,” a nod to the point of no return the industry crossed with agentic development.

Let’s walk through Rubicon from the top down. Many things stay the same: requests from Git clients, the API, and the web UI arrive at Spokes, which still handles routing and load balancing, and serves the internal API that the rest of GitHub uses to read and write repository data. Spokes also maintains its role as a placement and balancing service, maintaining the partitioning, isolation, and tenant fairness required for horizontal scale. The data plane underneath Spokes, in contrast, is new.

Spokes routes each request to one of a small group of virtual replicas assigned to that repository. Virtual replicas are a single fleet of disposable compute that serves reads, writes, or both. A virtual replica’s local disk is a cache that can be rebuilt at any time.

Spokes routes a repository’s traffic to the same virtual replicas when it can, which keeps their caches warm and latency low, but nothing about correctness depends on a request landing on any particular virtual replica. When a virtual replica goes away, the repository’s other virtual replicas keep serving, and a replacement fills its cache as traffic arrives.

When a repository gets busy, we add more virtual replicas. Separately, a fleet of compaction nodes works in the background, directly against Blob Storage and Ref-CAS, to merge each repository’s packs and remove unreachable objects. That work never runs on a virtual replica, meaning their CPU and memory can be almost entirely dedicated to serving requests.

Let’s walk the architecture from the perspective of a write and a read:

There are many distinct approaches we’ve taken within the new architecture, from the software stack on the virtual replicas to how traffic routing and workload scheduling are tuned for different use cases (e.g., many small repos vs. one large one). Let’s dive deep into two components of the architecture and tell their stories centered on the two design principles.

Object storage services like Azure Blob Storage are incredibly good at what they do, and natural fits for many data-heavy applications like source code storage. They are durable and replicated, hold as much as we need them to, have a relatively low cost per byte, and deliver exceptionally high aggregate throughput so long as traffic is spread across many keys. As a result, nearly everything durable in the new Git storage architecture lives in Azure Blob Storage, including:

Most of that data is written once to a distinct key and never changes afterward, which is what makes it highly suitable for Blob Storage.

References are the opposite. A busy repository’s references are a relatively small piece of state that many writes race to update at the same time. That access pattern is harder to serve from Blob Storage, and our first prototypes showed us why. Object storage for this kind of workload is an intuitive choice, and one that works well up to a certain level of scale: conditional writes give a compare-and-swap for free, and the data is already durable and replicated. However, we ran into a difficult set of choices, illustrated below by two approaches we tried.

Our first approach was a write-ahead log for all of a repository’s references, indexed by a single blob. Every update is a conditional write against that blob. It’s correct, and reads are cheap: a virtual replica asks whether the blob has changed, and if it hasn’t, it’s done. Writes are where it falls short. Only one conditional write to a blob can be in flight at a time, so even when we batch every push that arrives during a write into the next one, the repository’s write ceiling is one write per round trip to a single blob. In practice, this netted us write throughput in the mid-hundreds per second.

Our second approach split the references across several segment blobs, with each reference hashing to one of them. Writes to references in different segments no longer wait on each other, which raises the ceiling. But a read now has to check every segment to know whether anything changed, and it’s only as fast as the slowest segment. We’d traded write latency for read latency, and reads outnumber writes by orders of magnitude. We can visualize this by showing multiple writes and a read side by side:

Neither option met every requirement, so we built something else: a replicated log service dedicated to references, or the Ref-CAS service. This is the (small) consensus group we said at the end of the last post was still somewhere in the stack. Here’s how it works.

Each reference update in Ref-CAS is a compare-and-swap committed by a quorum. A transaction locks only the references it touches, checks that each one still has the value the writer expects, and becomes visible once a majority of the log’s replicas have recorded it. Agents mostly push to their own branches, so most updates don’t contend, and they replicate in parallel. When two pushes do update the same reference, only one will append to the log.

Each commit to Ref-CAS gets a log sequence number (LSN), and the repository’s version is the LSN of its latest commit. Reads start with a version check against that version. A virtual replica sends the version of the reference state it already holds. If that version is still current, the reply carries no references at all, and an unchanged repository costs a single comparison. If the virtual replica is behind, it gets back the current references and version, along with pointers to pack data and anything else that needs to be known, such as when the last garbage collection cycle occurred.

Unlike the consensus group in our legacy architecture, Ref-CAS never holds or validates object data. It only stores pointers to it. A virtual replica uploads a push’s pack to Blob Storage before it commits the reference transaction. A reference only ever points at objects that are already durable.

These approaches allow us to transact reference state alone in the tens of thousands of operations per second, and provide a baseline for additional annotations on the replication log that help reads decide what to download for a given request.

But supporting thousands of writes per second on a single repository creates a second-order problem: maintaining the performance of reads and writes. For that, we needed a different approach to compaction.

Every push adds a pack to Blob Storage. Git can serve a repository from many packs, but each additional pack makes lookups slightly more expensive, so the pack count can’t grow without bound. In our legacy architecture, compaction runs on the same replicas that serve traffic, and it has to run on every replica. In the new architecture, it runs entirely in the background on separate nodes, directly against durable storage, using the Ref-CAS service to notify virtual replicas of the evolving state.

Compaction nodes each claim ownership over a subset of the repository’s data via a lease, which allows us to scale compaction horizontally. Here’s how one compaction node works through a single shard of a repository’s packs:

The node first takes a lease on the shard in Blob Storage, so only one node compacts a shard at a time. Meanwhile, virtual replicas keep uploading packs and listing them in the pack manifest. The node reads the manifest to find new packs and collects them into an open segment, which is effectively a holding ground for data about to be compacted. When the segment reaches a defined limit based on pack count, size, or age, it seals the segment so it accepts no new packs, then combines its packs into fewer, larger ones. While compaction happens, the next segment for the shard can begin capturing its input packs. Before anything is published, the node checks that the new packs contain exactly the same objects as the inputs. It then uploads the new packs and adds them to the manifest. A second transaction removes the inputs from the manifest, and cleanup deletes them later.

To scale out, this process runs independently for many shards at once. Each pack’s hash maps to a shard, so adding shards and nodes adds compaction throughput. A compacted pack that’s still under the size limit goes back into a shard by its own hash and merges again, so packs grow in tiers until they reach the limit. The net result is an effectively flat pack count while pushes climb:

Compaction merges packs, but it keeps every object. Garbage collection is the step that removes objects nothing references anymore, like the ones left behind by deleted branches and force-pushes. In our new architecture, garbage collection works very similarly to compaction by using dedicated workers to handle the compute-heavy portion. It also has a few special behaviors for safely removing data, which are worthy of their own discussion.

Using these approaches, we’ve been able to consistently demonstrate a flat latency curve as writes climb, and we have other planned optimizations to make the compaction and garbage collection processes themselves more performant.

The two principles we started with let each part of this architecture scale on its own. We scale reads and writes by adding virtual replicas. Ref-CAS orders each reference independently instead of the whole repository. We scale maintenance by adding compaction nodes. Our goal is for every repository, from one developer’s side project to the busiest monorepo, to run on the same architecture with no special handling for the busy ones. Repositories don’t stay small. A side project can pick up a fleet of agents overnight, and it shouldn’t need a migration or special handling from us to keep up. One architecture also means every improvement we make reaches every repository at once.

This foundation lets us do a lot more. We’re focused on three areas next: scaling the architecture beyond what we can predict we’ll need today, making agents more efficient at the work they do on GitHub, and evolving Git itself for agentic development.

We have plans to take this architecture further. Each of these improvements lets a repository handle more activity while reducing the work behind each request:

Scaling the infrastructure is one side of supporting agents. The other side is making each agent operation cheaper: fewer bytes to fetch, fewer round trips, and less waiting on changes. Each of these fast-follow items builds on the storage work in this post:

We also have work to do in Git itself. GitHub has been a longtime contributor to the Git project, and we’re not stopping now. A few examples of the work we’re excited to continue:

Agents have changed how software gets built. GitHub and Git are changing with it. We’re excited to take this new architecture across the starting line. It’s available in a limited private preview now, with a broader rollout early next year. We can’t wait to see what teams build when the number of agents working on a repository is limited by their ideas. We’ll keep evolving Git and GitHub alongside our customers and the Git community.

Brian is a Principal Software Engineer working on the storage and core services that power all repository interactions on GitHub.

‘Relishing the Moment’: A Runner Ran 2:49 at the NYC Marathon—Dressed as a Hot Dog

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sarah Pezzino nearly gave up on running. Soon, she could own a Guinness World Record.

I ran Sunday's New York City Marathon in some of my finest FlipBelt gear and spent the final five miles dealing with cramps, a full stomach, and legs that were mad at me for taking on this course willingly for the third time. As for Sarah Pezzino, her race went a little differently.

The 23-year-old from Easton, Pennsylvania, showed up to the starting line in Staten Island dressed as a hot dog and finished in 2:49:15, which, after hearing, made me think I could’ve done a lot more than just iron my name onto my tank top.

Pezzino, a personal trainer and group fitness coach, was attempting to break a Guinness World Record for the fastest marathon dressed as a savory food. The previous mark was 3:11:34, which, incredibly, she beat by more than 22 minutes! Guinness still has to review the evidence before anything becomes official, but judging by the number of people who noticed her on Sunday, finding witnesses won't be a problem.

When speaking with Runner’s World, Pezzino said the hot dog wasn't even originally part of her marathon plans—and neither, for a while, was running another marathon at all.

Pezzino had been competing since childhood, starting in a local running series at age 4 before moving on to cross country and track in high school and college. She made her marathon debut in Chicago in 2024. She returned the following year, but a serious medical emergency at Mile 17 forced her to drop out. Afterward, she strongly considered giving up running altogether.

"I'd put too much pressure on myself and lost the joy in the sport," she told Runner's World.

A close friend helped convince her to give racing another chance, and they came up with a plan to run Chicago and New York in 2026, raise money for the Multiple Myeloma Research Foundation (MMRF), and get their friends and community involved along the way. With any luck, doing this would be a chance to enjoy running again without making every race about the clock.

Pezzino had a deep personal connection to the fundraising part of their plans. She lost her mother to a rare cancer in 2022, and Mike, the husband of a close family friend, died from multiple myeloma. She had partnered with MMRF for her first marathon and wanted to reconnect with the organization. With help from her coworkers at CycleFit Lehigh Valley, she organized everything from raffles and 6Ks to ice cream scooping and what they called “no-shower happy hours.” All these fundraising efforts brought in more than $8,000.

And sometime during all that planning, someone suggested she run New York as a hot dog.

A friend had spotted a costume at Target, so Pezzino went to see it for herself. She tried it on, filmed a video of herself running through the aisles, and sent it to the group. "The next thing I knew, I was walking out of Target with a hot dog costume," she recalled.

The Guinness idea came later, when she started looking into potential records and discovered the savory food category. By September, she'd committed to the attempt, despite the rather obvious logistical challenge of actually running 26.2 miles in the thing, which was labeled as suitable for ages 8 and up.

That left a lot of leeway. Pezzino said the body fit an adult, but the headpiece was considerably smaller, so she wore it as a hood instead. Someone from CycleFit helped tailor the sides, which helped.

Her total mileage running in the outfit before race day? Three miles, completed the day before.

Fortunately, New York's cool weather on race day worked in her favor, and she didn't experience the overheating or chafing that could've made for a miserable afternoon. She carried a Nathan handheld water bottle through the first 20 miles to help with fueling, and the costume itself gave her surprisingly little trouble.

"The biggest issue was getting it off afterward, when it was all sweaty," she said.

Race morning brought plenty of attention, even before she'd crossed the starting line. Runners asked whether she intended to wear the costume the entire way or ditch it with her throwaway clothes. Pezzino happily posed for pictures, and once she got moving, the commentary from spectators was about what you'd expect.

She heard plenty of "HOOOT DOOOG!" along the course, plus more than a few people yelling, "Look at that glizzy!"

Pezzino was enjoying herself enough that she barely thought about the record. Three weeks earlier, she'd run a personal best 2:45:57 in Chicago, but New York was an entirely different experience, with no thought to chasing another PR, even with the Guinness attempt on the line.

"In New York, my mind was nowhere near the clock," she said. "It was set on having fun, embracing the crowds, seeing the city, and taking the race mile by mile."

She spent much of the race chatting with other runners, jumping into photos, and enjoying the crowds rather than sweating her splits. And when she reached Central Park, she found herself celebrating a finish that would have seemed pretty unlikely a year earlier.

She'd even attracted some attention from a familiar and very famous face, who was serving as grand marshal for the event.

"Tracy Morgan saw me finish and said, 'I hope that hot dog is relishing this moment,'" Pezzino recalled.

Within seconds, Pezzino said, a broadcaster had pulled her aside for an interview and clipped a microphone onto the mustard portion of her costume. She was still trying to wrap her head around what she'd accomplished, which seems fair considering she'd just run a sub-three-hour marathon dressed as something you'd order at a baseball game.

Of everything that happened on Sunday, though, Pezzino's favorite moment came just before the finish, when she spotted her friends and CycleFit teammates cheering in the final 200 meters. These were the people who'd encouraged her to return to racing, helped with the fundraising, and supported her through two marathons in three weeks. Having them there as she finished meant more to her than any reaction to the costume.

"I didn't cross a single finish line this year alone," she said.

Sean Abrams was the Senior Editor, Growth and Engagement at Men’s Health. He’s a former hip hop dancer who likes long walks on the beach and large glasses of tequila. You can find his previous work at Maxim, Elite Daily, and AskMen.

American Airlines Has Reopened Its Exclusive LAX Dining Room After 6 Years—Here’s How To Get In

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Last Wednesday I wrote exclusively American Airlines would reopen its Flagship Dining inside the business class lounge at LAX this week. Now it’s officially open. What was First Class Dining has been closed since March 2020, and when it returns, qualifying American business class passengers will be able to use it with their tickets, and there are other paths to entry too. That’s great news. When it was open previously it was fantastic. Flagship First Dining was a restaurant inside the Flagship Lounge. There were tables, booths, a bar and a window looking out toward the aircraft. The room had 59 seats, but when I visited there were never more than two other passengers at once. The food was excellent, too. I especially liked the miso black cod, and the pork belly came with cauliflower tempura that I still remember. American has since dropped “First” from the name. As the airline removes international first class from its Boeing 777-300ERs and replaces its three cabin Airbus A321Ts, they’ve opened the dining rooms to a much larger group instead of doing away with them. Since September 15, qualifying Flagship Business and Flagship Business Plus passengers have been able to use Flagship Dining in Dallas and Miami. Los Angeles is next. Access covers qualifying American Airlines long haul international, premium transcontinental and Flagship Hawaii itineraries. That includes eligible Flagship flights between Los Angeles and New York JFK, though an ordinary domestic first class ticket doesn’t get you in. The qualifying flight must be marketed and operated by American. Business class access doesn’t include a guest. Getting into the Flagship Lounge based on AAdvantage or oneworld status doesn’t get you into the separate dining room. Eligible partner first class access continues, and there’s also a way in with miles. AAdvantage Platinum Pro members can redeem 25,000 miles and Executive Platinum members can redeem 20,000 miles for a Flagship Dining pass. It includes the member and their immediate family or up to two guests on an eligible same day American or oneworld flight, even when flying coach. It includes the Flagship Lounge, too. I’ve thought this was one of the best uses of miles when traveling as a family. American has introduced a new dining menu and a wine and chocolate pairing featuring KHK Hillside Cabernet Sauvignon and K+M chocolate, co-founded by chef Thomas Keller. The cabernet retails for about $125 a bottle. Here’s American’s photo of the pairing. The new Dallas wine list also includes Bollinger La Grande Année, which retails for around $200 a bottle. American even listed it as an ingredient in a cranberry orange spritz. That seems like an expensive way to make it harder to taste the champagne. I’d ask for it in a separate glass. And the food has improved in the broader Flagship lounges, not just in the separate dining rooms. On a recent visit to the Dallas Flagship Lounge, I found a better buffet, a staffed charcuterie station and an improved chef station. I ordered from the lounge’s menu using a QR code, and the food arrived at my table in four minutes. It was a relatively quiet Tuesday evening, but I was impressed. That menu ordering is available in Los Angeles, too. When I last wrote about the rollout, the QR codes were in the theater seating and across from the secondary beverage station. Delta still offers the more ambitious food. It now has two Delta One lounges at LAX, with table service throughout, while United’s Polaris Lounge has a sit down restaurant. I’d still put the food in American’s main Flagship Lounge behind Delta, even though the gap has narrowed. But American makes the larger Flagship Lounge available to many more passengers, including eligible elites traveling in coach. And now a regular qualifying Flagship Business fare comes with the separate dining room. United’s base Polaris fares don’t include Polaris Lounge access. Delta’s Basic Business fares will lose Delta One lounge access for travel beginning January 19, 2027. At this point Americna’s three Flagship Dining locations really close a gap and even start to offer a competitive advantage for some. The tradeoff is that First Dining was so special because almost nobody was there. Opening it to business class is a big improvement for those passengers, but it also means many more people competing for a table. American uses an electronic waitlist when the dining room is full. If you’re traveling before a busy departure period, I’d check in with the host when you first arrive. What was First Class Dining has been closed since March 2020, and when it returns, qualifying American business class passengers will be able to use it with their tickets, and there are other paths to entry too. That’s great news. When it was open previously it was fantastic.

Flagship First Dining was a restaurant inside the Flagship Lounge. There were tables, booths, a bar and a window looking out toward the aircraft. The room had 59 seats, but when I visited there were never more than two other passengers at once.

The food was excellent, too. I especially liked the miso black cod, and the pork belly came with cauliflower tempura that I still remember.

American has since dropped “First” from the name. As the airline removes international first class from its Boeing 777-300ERs and replaces its three cabin Airbus A321Ts, they’ve opened the dining rooms to a much larger group instead of doing away with them.

Since September 15, qualifying Flagship Business and Flagship Business Plus passengers have been able to use Flagship Dining in Dallas and Miami. Los Angeles is next.

Access covers qualifying American Airlines long haul international, premium transcontinental and Flagship Hawaii itineraries. That includes eligible Flagship flights between Los Angeles and New York JFK, though an ordinary domestic first class ticket doesn’t get you in. The qualifying flight must be marketed and operated by American. Business class access doesn’t include a guest.

Getting into the Flagship Lounge based on AAdvantage or oneworld status doesn’t get you into the separate dining room. Eligible partner first class access continues, and there’s also a way in with miles.

AAdvantage Platinum Pro members can redeem 25,000 miles and Executive Platinum members can redeem 20,000 miles for a Flagship Dining pass. It includes the member and their immediate family or up to two guests on an eligible same day American or oneworld flight, even when flying coach. It includes the Flagship Lounge, too. I’ve thought this was one of the best uses of miles when traveling as a family.

American has introduced a new dining menu and a wine and chocolate pairing featuring KHK Hillside Cabernet Sauvignon and K+M chocolate, co-founded by chef Thomas Keller. The cabernet retails for about $125 a bottle. Here’s American’s photo of the pairing.

The new Dallas wine list also includes Bollinger La Grande Année, which retails for around $200 a bottle. American even listed it as an ingredient in a cranberry orange spritz. That seems like an expensive way to make it harder to taste the champagne. I’d ask for it in a separate glass.

And the food has improved in the broader Flagship lounges, not just in the separate dining rooms. On a recent visit to the Dallas Flagship Lounge, I found a better buffet, a staffed charcuterie station and an improved chef station. I ordered from the lounge’s menu using a QR code, and the food arrived at my table in four minutes. It was a relatively quiet Tuesday evening, but I was impressed.

That menu ordering is available in Los Angeles, too. When I last wrote about the rollout, the QR codes were in the theater seating and across from the secondary beverage station.

Delta still offers the more ambitious food. It now has two Delta One lounges at LAX, with table service throughout, while United’s Polaris Lounge has a sit down restaurant. I’d still put the food in American’s main Flagship Lounge behind Delta, even though the gap has narrowed.

But American makes the larger Flagship Lounge available to many more passengers, including eligible elites traveling in coach. And now a regular qualifying Flagship Business fare comes with the separate dining room.

United’s base Polaris fares don’t include Polaris Lounge access. Delta’s Basic Business fares will lose Delta One lounge access for travel beginning January 19, 2027. At this point Americna’s three Flagship Dining locations really close a gap and even start to offer a competitive advantage for some.

The tradeoff is that First Dining was so special because almost nobody was there. Opening it to business class is a big improvement for those passengers, but it also means many more people competing for a table. American uses an electronic waitlist when the dining room is full. If you’re traveling before a busy departure period, I’d check in with the host when you first arrive.

Portuguese Journalist Makes Bombshell Claim About Cristiano Ronaldo’s Retirement

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The farewell Cristiano Ronaldo had envisioned for when he finally waved goodbye to international football with Portugal hasn't quite gone like the fairytale many expected.

Having hoped to hang up his international boots with a World Cup under his belt, things instead ended in tears for the 41-year-old, who spent the final month of his international career at loggerheads with new manager Jorge Jesus, received a fine for his public outburst and was subsequently handed a ban.

The Euro 2016 winner seemingly left it too late to call time on his international career, finding it increasingly difficult to accept that he was no longer the same player who had claimed five Ballon d'Or titles during his spells at Real Madrid and Manchester United.

But now, his farewell could be set for an even more sorrowful twist, should the bombshell claims made by a Portuguese journalist about plans for his send-off prove to be true.

Journalist Pedro Sepulveda told talkSPORT that Jesus had intended to call-up Ronaldo during the next international break, in an attempt to rebuild bridges, after the Al-Nassr superstar was left fuming over a decision that saw him benched during a 2-1 win against Norway and did not come on.

Sepulveda now claims that Ronaldo is still so caught up in that drama that the then-captain will be blocked from having a farewell match, though it might be because he doesn't want to either. He said:

“I can guarantee you that Ronaldo won't dress officially, let's say like that, in the Portuguese jersey again. I can guarantee you that until Jorge Jesus leaves the national team of Portugal that last friendly game for everyone to say goodbye to Cristiano Ronaldo won't happen.”

Ronaldo's exit comes after he dramatically fell out with Portugal boss Jorge Jesus.

He added: “The thing is, Ronaldo feels that he wanted to retire after the World Cup. He made that decision and then Pedro Proenca, the new president of the federation, with Jorge Jesus, they both convinced him to stay. In the head of Ronaldo, after they convinced him, they took the decision to make this to Ronaldo without defending him.”

That would be in stark contrast to his Argentine adversary, Lionel Messi, who played his 208th and final international match in Buenos Aires in October.

Interestingly, the journalist's comments seem to contradict the Portugal FA's initial statement, which concludes: “Cristiano Ronaldo has elevated Portugal’s name and inspired generations of young people who dream of following in his footsteps, being, for many of us, the best player of all time!

“Therefore, the Portuguese Football Federation refuses to let this farewell take place without due tribute. It is our firm and immense desire to organise, plan, participate and actively collaborate in what will always be a fitting tribute to Cristiano Ronaldo’s immense career, in which fans and the country can applaud and celebrate, once again, the player who has given us so much.”

A date, time, and official agreement has not yet been reached as rumours of Ronaldo's desire not to take part in another game continuing to flutter. But as Portugal's greatest-ever player, and up there among the best players in world football, it would surely be inconceivable that CR7 doesn't get a proper goodbye.

Mortgage Rates Edge Down for Third Week

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

wood block of percentage change to UP and Down arrow. Interest rate, GDP, stocks market, financial, deflation and Inflation, ranking, mortgage, debt, Loan and Economic Recession concept

Mortgage rates came down for the third-straight week, but whether the recent trend is a sign of things to come remains unknown, although economists point toward any progress in a resolution in the Middle East as a step in the right direction.

The latest Freddie Mac Primary Mortgage Market Survey® released Thursday shows the average 30-year fixed-rate mortgage rate at 7.18%, down six basis points from 7.24% last week. The 30-year FRM is still up year-over-year from 6.22% last year. The 15-year FRM averaged 6.57% down from 6.60% last week. A year ago at this time, the 15-year FRM averaged 5.50%.

At presstime, the average rate was 7.17% Thursday afternoon, according to Mortgage News Daily, about one percentage point above year-ago levels.

Realtor.com Senior Economist Hannah Jones said Tuesday’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey came in stronger than expected, with layoffs down slightly and a significant gain in job openings, which points to a potentially strong jobs report tomorrow.

“While solid hiring supports housing demand over time, a stronger-than-expected report could also lift bond yields and slow the pace of rate declines in the near term,” she cautioned.

Regarding Tuesday’s midterm elections which gave Democrats a majority in both the House and Senate, Jones said it is not yet clear whether the shift will be felt in the housing market.

“Many newly elected representatives have voiced discontent with the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, which has contributed to rising inflation, so any progress toward resolution could mean lower inflation and softer mortgage rates ahead,” she said.

Although rates have eased over the last few weeks, they remain above 7%, keeping many buyers on the sidelines. Realtor.com’s October housing market report showed another month of a slowing housing market, with both buyers and sellers pulling back. Pending sales fell 10.5% year over year; new-home sales fell 0.6% on a monthly basis in September, which Realtor.com® Senior Economist Joel Berner characterized as “muted” for this year.

“Still, sellers aren’t delisting in droves, which suggests they are disengaging rather than acting out of distress,” Jones added. “With 43% of active listings having had a price reduction, buyers who can afford to participate are in a relatively strong position, facing less competition and more price cuts.”

MFA Boston to Allow Visitors to Watch Live Conservation of Dutch Painting

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nicolaes Eliasz Pickenoy’s 17th-century group portrait of an Amsterdam wine guild will be the star of a pop-up restoration studio that museumgoers can peer into.

Visitors to the Museum of Fine Arts (MFA) in Boston can soon watch the year-long restoration process of Nicolaes Eliasz Pickenoy's "The Founders of the Amsterdam Wine Merchants Guild" (1625).

Beginning on December 9, museumgoers can watch as conservators, scientists, art historians, and curators cycle through a pop-up studio in the MFA's Center for Netherlandish Art's Innovation Gallery.

Acquired last year, the group portrait depicts the six founding members of the lucrative Amsterdam Wine Merchants Guild, which operated until the late 18th century.

“It’s about ambition, concentration, desire” – Amorim tears into Milan after Ferencvaros defeat

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

AC Milan head coach Ruben Amorim did not hold back on his team’s performance after the 2-0 Europa League defeat to Ferencvaros.

Milan needed a win having lost two of their first three games in Europe, and instead they responded with perhaps their worst performance under Amorim. Lenny Joseph’s second-half brace was enough for Ferencvaros to emerge with three points that their performance merited in truth.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The decision was made to rotate again with six changes from the win over Inter, but in the second half the likes of Christian Pulisic, Diego Moreira, Mario Gila and Luka Modric all came on. The end result was the same: frustration and an inability to piece together the required quality.

Amorim spoke to Sky after Koni De Winter offered his opinion that it is a psychological issue for the team. The words from the manager were transcribed by MilanNews.

You issued a warning that today would be complication…

“There’s no explanation. There’s nothing I can say, especially about the first half. I don’t remember another first half like it. It’s about ambition, concentration, desire. We gave away opportunities, we gave away stupid gifts. We weren’t prepared to play football tonight.”

“It’s hard to explain. We simply weren’t ready to move from the derby to this other competition. When you play for Milan, you have a responsibility, and you can’t show up like that. I probably didn’t ensure the team took to the field ready either.”

De Winter says it’s a matter of mentality…

“We’re not ready to be competitive on two fronts. We need to work on our way of looking at football and how we approach matches. There are no excuses tonight, there’s nothing I can say.

“I usually have a lot to say in defence of our team, but not tonight. We need to be more humble and understand what happened.”

“Maybe I did something wrong these past two days, but not mentally. This team had similar problems last year. We have to find a solution. Tonight we weren’t ready, we weren’t brave. If we don’t win, we’ll have to do everything we can to turn things around, and we didn’t see that.

“Everyone needs to train like Pavlovic. He’s ready to die in training. He’s a good example. Sometimes when players don’t start, they struggle. But there are no excuses; we should have won even with those who started.”

“We have to fix this! We can’t just assume it’s going to be like this. We should have done so many things better tonight. Just being better on second balls would have been much better. We weren’t in the game tonight.”

How important is the Europa League now? It’s a complicated situation…

“I have to focus on the league, obviously, but we also have to do well in the Europa League. We must and can win games despite the rotation. We have to be ready – we’re not now – to play and win every three days.

“But I won’t give up on this competition. But as long as the mathematics don’t condemn us, we’ll fight in this competition as we do in the others.”

Former McDonald's Chef Reveals Why Fast Food Chicken Tenders Are Smaller Than They Look

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

From accusations of serving heavily processed food to concerns about the temperature of scalding coffee, fast food chains have faced their fair share of backlash over the years. One thing they could usually fall back on was offering an affordable meal, but with soaring prices, that promise is becoming harder to keep. And while customers have certainly felt the rising costs, they've not noticed a corresponding increase in quality. In fact, some are beginning to question whether they're really getting any bang for their buck at all.

Enter shrinkflation, the increasingly common practice of charging more while quietly reducing portion sizes. Even more frustratingly, some chains might be selling you a product that looks perfectly generous on the outside but contains surprisingly little actual meat. In a documentary produced by More Perfect Union, former McDonald's corporate chef Mike Haracz revealed some industry tricks fast food chains use to make their chicken tenders look bigger than they actually are. Right off the bat, Haracz explained why some chains compromise on product quality in the first place. "They are willing to serve not as good of a product because they can make more money and serve more people," he shared. "Make it as cheap as possible, so they can get the most profit."

So how exactly do they pull it off? According to Haracz, there's more than one way to make a chicken tender like bigger without actually adding more chicken. One approach involves the marinade. By injecting or soaking the meat in a saltwater brine, manufacturers can increase its moisture content and weight, making the tender appear plumper. Then there's the breading. Using a thicker coating, double-dredging the chicken, or opting for bulkier panko breadcrumbs can all add extra volume to the finished product.

It's no secret that nobody likes shrinkflation. Just look at the brutal way those accused of it were treated in the Middle Ages. These days, however, mob justice has largely been replaced by people venting their frustrations on Reddit. Reacting to the More Perfect Union video, one Redditor wrote, "Any company that is publicly traded has a fiduciary duty to sell you the worst possible product for the most money possible. Fast food is no exception." This user might be onto something since chicken tenders aren't the only fast food product that has been accused of shrinkflation.

According to Fox News, chicken is the number one best-selling fast food protein on DoorDash, with beef burgers not far behind. While anything labeled 100% ground beef legally cannot contain added water, and coating it in extra breading is obviously out of the question, that doesn't mean chains are short on other tricks to cut costs and make their beef go further. According to the More Perfect Union documentary, one common approach is to use beef with a lower lean-to-fat ratio, which is typically cheaper than leaner blends. However, the price trade-off becomes apparent once the patty hits the grill.

As the fat renders out during cooking, the burger can shrink considerably, leaving you with a smaller finished patty than you might expect from its raw weight — and it's the raw weight that most fast food joints use in advertising. Another trick involves the toppings themselves. By reducing the size of ingredients like lettuce, tomatoes, pickles, and even the bun, restaurants can make them more proportionate to a smaller patty, helping maintain the illusion of a generously filled burger even when there's less meat underneath.

Georgia O’Keeffe Painting Found in Closet After 40 Years Heads to Christie’s With $ 3 M. Est.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

When Christie’s holds its 20th Century Evening Sale in two weeks, one painting will hit the block with a curious story. The Georgia O’Keeffe painting Near Abiquiu, New Mexico I (1930) only recently resurfaced after nearly 40 years out of the public eye.

Per the New York Post, the painting was found by a Rhode Island woman when she was helping her elderly aunt clear her East Village apartment. According to the 65-year-old woman, who the Post names only by her middle name, Betsy, she found the painting at the back of a closet under a row of fur coats. Related Articles Gloria Steinem's Life in Art What We Miss When We Flatten Georgia O'Keeffe Into a Feminist Icon

“I thought, wow, that’s that’s pretty wild,” she told the Post. “I wondered, ‘Is there any way I can keep this?’ … But once we started exploring what the value of the piece was, I realized that it’s worth more than my house.”

According to the woman, her aunt told her in 2022 off-handedly that there was an O’Keeffe painting in the closet as they prepped the house for home health aides. However, Betsy didn’t actually find the work until a few weeks ago when her aunt was put into hospice at age 98.

The aunt, who has since died, was once a Foreign Services worker. She recieved the painting as a graduation gift from her mother in 1963, when she purchased it from The Downtown Gallery on 51st Street for $3,800. As Betsy tells it, the painting hung in the apartment until the 1980s, when she noticed a guest “eyeing it a bit too closely.”

“I think she was fully aware of its value … she recognized that it was an expression of her financial position,” Betsy said.

The painting has since become a bit of an oddity for Betsy and her family, who says she hung it up “for chuckles.” “The men in the family who’ve seen this have commented, there seems to be a number of ‘vaginas’ in the picture,” she said

The painting carries a $2 million to $3 million estimate, and a portion of the proceeds will be donated to an unnamed educaiton institution. The rest will go towards Betsy and her family “for things like paying off the mortgage.” Fair enough.

In a lot essay, Christie’s described the painting as an “important early work” that is “likely the first iteration in a series of three works depicting Lobo Peak, north of Taos Pueblo.” One of the other such works is in the collection of MoMA, while the other is in an unamed private collection.

Top Democrats call to rein in AI buildout after midterms sweep: 'National data center moratorium now'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After a big win in the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, some Democrats are already signaling they want to take on the artificial build-out, specifically data centers.

In a post on X, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., touted referendums across the U.S. on Tuesday where voters in localities approved restrictions on data centers. Sanders, who caucuses with the Democrats, said the message to political leaders is clear.

"National data center moratorium now," he wrote in the post. "Americans don't want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity's future."

In another post on X, Rep. Yassamin Ansari, D-Ariz., wrote that pausing AI development needs to be a top priority for the large Democratic majority in the House on Day 1. Rep. Greg Casar, D-Texas, the chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus, amplified the post and said, "Yes!"

According to the CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said AI and data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision at the ballot box. Of those voters, 65% said they cast a ballot for a Democratic candidate for Congress.

Stocks linked to the artificial intelligence build-out declined slightly on Wednesday in the wake of the election, but the selling intensified on Thursday as investors worried Democratic wins could have a real impact on data center construction. Chip stocks led the decline. The iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) tumbled nearly 9% on Tuesday. Nvidia declined 8%, while Advanced Micro Devices sunk more than 14%.

Democrats who flipped critical races on Tuesday also singled out big tech companies and data centers.

South Carolina Democrat Annie Andrews, who flipped a Senate seat in the state on Tuesday, launched a website during the campaign that branded her opponent — Sen. Darline Graham — "data center Darline."

In her victory speech on Tuesday, Andrews said voters chose a South Carolina "where decisions about communities are made by the people who live in our communities, not the billionaires or data center companies in Washington D.C."

Ohio Democrat Sherrod Brown, who is heading back to the Senate after defeating incumbent Republican Sen. Jon Husted on Tuesday, said in his victory speech, "I do not work for data centers."

Democratic governors also attacked data centers in their victory speeches. Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro, who signed an executive order in August to slow data center development, said big tech companies shouldn't determine residents' futures. "Instead, the folks in our local communities get the final say," he said.

Perhaps most important to the data center build-out is Texas, which is home to 27% of planned data center capacity, according to Wells Fargo.

Gina Hinojosa, the Democrat who ousted incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, didn't address data centers directly in her victory speech, though they played a key role in her campaign.

"When I am governor, I will issue a moratorium, a ban, on all data center approvals until laws can be passed by the legislature to protect Texans," she said in a video posted to her X in late October. "This is about one fight. Is this going to be a Texas that is by and for the people, or is this the billionaire's world and we just live here? Data centers are part of that fight."

The 6 Best Airplane Bathrooms On Any Airline: The A380 Reigns Supreme

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

When most people think of airplane bathrooms, it’s probably not a nice image that comes to mind. Airlines have largely shrunk bathrooms on newer aircraft significantly, and at times they’re also not very well maintained.

That’s not true across the board, though. In premium cabins, we’ve seen airlines introduce some pretty amazing amenities in recent years, and that even includes awesome bathrooms.

In this post, I’d like to share what I consider to be the airlines with the best first class bathrooms (somewhat overlapping with my list of the world’s best first class airlines).

Unsurprisingly, the Airbus A380 takes center stage on this flight, as it’s an aircraft that has ushered in the most bathroom innovation. That’s because the front of the upper deck has a bit of extra space that can’t be utilized for seats, making room for some cool amenities.

With that in mind, below are my six favorite airplane bathrooms, roughly ranked…

Emirates A380 first class is one of my all-around favorite first class products, and a large part of that is the amazing A380 first class shower suites.

Emirates’ A380s have two first class shower suites at the front of the upper deck, equipped with heated floors, enough amenities to start a toiletry resale business, and most importantly, a shower.

As a first class passenger, you can schedule a shower at the beginning of your flight, and while there’s no time limit to how long you can use the shower spa, you’re limited to five minutes of water. Fortunately you can turn the water on and off without the clock “running,” so that’s more than enough water for a nice shower.

Of all the excitement I’ve experienced in the air, I don’t think anything compares to the first time I took a shower on Emirates. The airline even has shower spa attendants, who make sure the shower spas are well maintained throughout the flight.

Etihad A380 first class consists of nine “First Class Apartments,” and these passengers have access to the shower suite at the front of the cabin. Much like on Emirates, each passenger gets five minutes of water, and the shower suite is refreshed between each use.

While this is a great and refreshing feature, the shower suite definitely isn’t as spacious as on Emirates. So if you’re used to showering on Emirates, you might find that it’s a bit tight, but otherwise you’ll no doubt be delighted. Also keep in mind that there’s still The Residence on Etihad, which is a three room suite that can be reserved as an upgrade over the standard first class. This comes with a private bathroom, including shower.

Singapore Airlines A380 Suites is an incredible first class product, with only six seats in the cabin. While you won’t find a shower on Singapore Airlines’ A380s, you will find some of the most spacious bathrooms in the sky. One of the lavatories has a huge vanity, and is probably the size of 10 typical Boeing 737 MAX bathrooms. Airplane bathrooms don’t get much more luxurious than this!

Qatar Airways A380 first class has two very nice lavatories at the front of the upper deck. While there aren’t any special amenities, you’ll find that they’re incredibly spacious, and nicely decorated.

Lufthansa A380 first class features two huge lavatories at the front of the upper deck, each equipped with a bench, and most excitingly, a urinal! Lufthansa is the only airline to offer urinals on planes, so that’s pretty special… or something.

While we’re speaking of Lufthansa, I have a soft spot for Lufthansa 747-8 first class, probably at least partly because the Boeing 747-8 is my favorite plane as an avgeek. While not quite as over the top as some of the other options, Lufthansa’s 747-8 lavatories are spacious, have a bench you can extend out over the toilet, and most importantly, have a window. It’s so nice to be able to look out the window while in the lavatory.

Lastly on the topic of Lufthansa and lavatories, I do have to bring up how cool the economy lavatories on the carrier’s A340-600s were (these planes were just recently retired). They were located on the “lower deck” (in a cargo compartment), so they were probably the most unusual lavatories you could’ve recently found in commercial service. By comparison, the Lufthansa A340 first class lavatories weren’t much to get excited about at all.

All Nippon Airways A380 first class has two large, identical lavatories at the front of the upper deck. They’re huge, but there are also a couple of thoughtful design touches that I appreciate about them.

For one, ANA has a bidet function for its toilets, which is always a nice feature to have. I also appreciate how there’s a “plank” of sorts that you can extend on the ground, for situations where you want to change into pajamas without touching the regular floor of the bathroom.

On top of that, ANA does an amazing job with keeping its lavatories clean between each use, down to folding the toilet paper into perfect triangles.

Now, in fairness, there are a decent number of products with a bidet function in service, from Japan Airlines A350 first class, to Gulf Air 787 business class, to Oman Air 787 business class.

There are some pretty incredible airplane bathrooms out there. While nothing can compete with Emirates’ amazing shower suites, there are plenty of other impressive airplane bathrooms out there as well, with everything from vanities, to bidets, to urinals. As A380s increasingly get retired, unfortunately going to the bathroom on a plane is getting less pleasant!

What’s your favorite bathroom on a commercial aircraft?

This Old-School Drink Is Making a Comeback for the Coziest Winter Cocktails

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Port isn't just for sipping after dinner. We asked a respected winemaker about how this rich, fruity wine can be used to make easy winter cocktails.

For my birthday, a friend gifted me a bottle of Angel’s Envy bourbon. It was a fantastic gift (thank you, Jaclyn!), but what stood out to me was a detail on the label: “finished in port wine barrels.”

Usually I hear of bourbon barrels being used to age other things. The Goose Island Bourbon County Stout, for example, is a beer that’s aged in bourbon barrels, and it’s so special that people will line up outside grocery stores in the hope of grabbing a pack. But I’m less familiar with port wine, and I wanted to learn more.

I’ve been seeing port pop up on menus and in cocktail recipes with more frequency. When I think of port, I imagine the nobility in medieval times sipping from big pewter goblets. Of course, you don’t need to be a royal to enjoy it!

I reached out to an expert for some advice on how to incorporate port into more of my winter cocktails. He gave me a crash course in everything I needed to know about the wine, and shared some of the best ways to use it in cocktails.

I connected with David Guimaraens, head winemaker for Taylor Fladgate. Born in Portugal, David is the sixth generation of his family to be involved in the port business. He’s widely recognized as one of the most gifted winemakers in the modern port industry, and his tips on how to winterize cocktails with port are simple enough to incorporate into any cocktail routine.

Taste of Home: For someone who may be unfamiliar with the category, how would you explain what port is and what distinguishes it from other styles of wine?

David Guimaraens: Port is a fortified wine produced in Portugal’s Douro Valley, one of the world’s oldest demarcated wine regions. What sets it apart is that a small amount of grape spirit is added during fermentation. This stops the fermentation process so some of the natural sweetness from the grapes remains in the finished product. The result is a uniquely rich, concentrated fruit wine with an incredible depth of flavor.

There are many different styles of port, from fresh, vibrant ruby ports to complex, aged tawnies and exceptional vintage ports. That diversity makes port a far more versatile category than many people realize, with styles suited to everything from cocktails and aperitifs to food pairings and after-dinner enjoyment.

TOH: What qualities does port bring to a mixed drink, and why does it work particularly well as a cocktail ingredient?

DG: Port brings fruit notes, sweetness and complexity to a cocktail. Depending on the style, its flavor profile can range from fresh berry and spice to dried fruit, nuts and caramel. It is remarkably versatile behind the bar. Port can replace or complement vermouth and other sweet components in classic cocktails. Its concentration of flavor allows you to add complexity to a drink without a long list of ingredients.

TOH: Why is port trending for winter cocktails and seasonal entertaining?

DG: There is something naturally comforting about port during the colder months. Its fruit and spice notes pair particularly well with seasonal flavors such as cranberry, orange, cinnamon, ginger and chocolate, making it a versatile choice for holiday entertaining. Port has also always been a wine for sharing. Serving it in a cocktail or a simple spritz offers a more informal, contemporary way to enjoy that tradition and bring port to the table.

TOH: For someone experimenting with port cocktails at home for the first time, what is the easiest way to begin? Are there simple serves, ratios or ingredient pairings you recommend?

DG: Keep it simple. You don’t need to be a mixologist to make a great port cocktail. A port spritz is an easy place to start: Serve port over plenty of ice, top with sparkling water or tonic, and finish with a slice or twist of fresh citrus. It is refreshing, simple to prepare and easy to adapt to your own taste.

TOH: For consumers shopping for port to use in cocktails, what should they look for when selecting a bottle? Does someone need to invest in an expensive Port to make a great cocktail?

DG: Absolutely not. In fact, I would reserve great vintage ports for enjoying on their own!

For cocktails, look for a good-quality, fruit-driven reserve ruby port with enough character to stand up alongside the other ingredients. Fonseca Bin 27 is a great example: It is approachable and full of rich berry fruit, and it has the structure and depth to work extremely well in cocktails.

Have you made port cocktails before? Let us know your favorite version in the comments below!

Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

An attacker drained Mercado Bitcoin-linked deposit addresses nearly four hours after the Brazilian exchange said it had contained a security breach, new blockchain research shows.

Bitquery's analysis, updated Thursday, traces more than $48 million in stolen assets across 11 blockchains. Its findings challenge the exchange's claim that it stopped the attack quickly.

The Bitcoin exchange announced at 9:56 a.m. Brazil time on November 2 that it had quickly contained the incident. Bitquery says 36,042 Ethereum customer deposit addresses were emptied between 9:27 a.m. and 1:56 p.m. Brazil time.

Those addresses held roughly $266,000, a small fraction of the total theft. But the attacker could still move those funds after the exchange’s announcement.

A customer deposit address works much like a personal receiving account for cryptocurrency. The exchange later gathers those coins into a shared online wallet, called a hot wallet, to process everyday transactions. Moving funds from a deposit address requires its private key, the secret that authorizes transfers.

Bitquery says the withdrawals were authorized directly by the deposit addresses, suggesting the attacker had access to their private keys or to a system capable of signing transactions on their behalf.

“That needs a key for each address, or a way into the system that signs for them,” the researchers explained. They warned that exchanges typically generate deposit keys from a single master key, meaning the attacker may still be able to access other customer addresses.

The amount stolen is also disputed. Initial estimates put the amount above $40 million, while Mercado Bitcoin's updated FAQ now puts affected assets at about $36.3 million. Bitquery estimates that $48.6 million was stolen across 11 blockchains.

Mercado Bitcoin's public updates have not explained the gap between its containment claim and the later transfers. The company assured customers it would cover user losses with its own funds.

Bitquery warns customers against reusing old deposit addresses until the exchange confirms they are safe. Mercado Bitcoin's reopening plan begins November 9 with balance viewing only; deposits, withdrawals and trading have no restart date.

Your Level Might Be the Wrong Size, and It Could Be Throwing Off Your Work

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A short level works for some projects, but longer surfaces need a wider check. Learn how to pick the right size and use it accurately.

Levels come in many different shapes and sizes, and figuring out how long a level should be for a certain project isn’t always straightforward. Choosing the right size matters because a level that’s too short can miss uneven spots, while one that’s too long can be difficult to use in tight spaces.

Here’s how much of a surface a level should cover, which sizes work best, and when overhang is okay.

It depends on the project, but a longer level is usually better for checking bigger surfaces. A shorter one might show that the middle of a board is level without catching a problem closer to either end.

The level doesn’t have to stretch across the entire surface, though. A 24-inch level can work on a four-foot shelf, for example, as long as the installer checks several spots along its length. For longer surfaces, a smaller level can sit on a long, straight board to check a wider area. A laser level is another option, especially when lining up things across a room.

The way the level sits on the material matters, too. A little sawdust or dirt underneath can affect the reading, so both surfaces should be clean. The tool also needs to sit firmly without rocking.

There’s no set rule for how much of a surface a level needs to cover, but it should fit comfortably and have enough contact to stay steady.

Different sizes work better for different jobs:

“Use a 4-foot carpenter’s level for framing whenever you have room for its length,” according to The Home Depot. “Warps or waves in the boards can throw off a shorter carpenter’s level.”

Another common concern is whether the level can hang over the edge of whatever is being checked. Some overhang is fine, provided the tool has enough support to stay steady.

If it starts tipping or rocking, a shorter level will likely work better. The bubble doesn’t need to be directly above the material, either. It can still give an accurate reading even when that part of the tool hangs past the edge.

Trump’s White House Ballroom Reaches a Major Construction Milestone

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The president signed the final steel beam for the 90,000-square-foot structure, which is slated to seat about 1,000 guests beneath 40-foot ceilings.

Construction on President Donald Trump’s White House ballroom continues to progress in the wake of the Supreme Court’s August ruling that allowed the once-stalled project to proceed. The structure has now officially topped out, meaning it’s reached its final height. On Wednesday, the day after the midterm elections, Trump invited reporters inside the 90,000-square-foot building as he signed the final structural steel beam before workers hoisted it into place.

The $600 million project, which sits on the site of the now-demolished East Wing, has a height of 51 feet and will include two above-ground floors, plus a new underground national security center. The Executive Residence portion of the White House reaches a height of 70 feet at its tallest point, the South Portico, although the majority of the structure is shorter.

Architect Shalom Baranes’s design closely aligns with the White House’s neoclassical style, with traditional colonnades, arches, and cornice lines. The centerpiece will be an elaborate 22,000-square-foot ballroom with a seating capacity of about 1,000. Trump has repeatedly claimed that the lavishly gilded space is set to have ceiling heights that reach 40 feet. Additionally, he revealed on Wednesday that marble for the floors had been selected and ordered.

The building is slated to hold office space for the First Lady, who traditionally kept an office in the East Wing, as well as a new movie theater to replace the one formerly located in the now-razed East Wing Colonnade.

Since taking office, Trump has proposed myriad construction projects in the nation’s capital, from a 250-foot-tall triumphal arch across the Potomac River from the Lincoln Memorial to a helipad on the South Lawn of the White House. The ballroom is expected to reach completion prior to the end of Trump’s term in January 2029.

It’s Interesting That Neither Sony Nor Tamron Have Said Anything Regarding Acquisition

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It’s Interesting That Neither Sony Nor Tamron Have Said Anything Regarding Acquisition

In late July, news broke that Sony was trying to acquire Tamron, a fact Tamron itself confirmed shortly after. However, since then, it’s been crickets.

Both Sony and Tamron released their financial results as scheduled today, a date I circled on my calendar in hopes that one or both companies might comment again on Sony’s attempted takeover of Tamron. However, neither company said anything about it at all. Sometimes the lack of news is itself news.

Back in July, Tamron said it had established a “special committee” to explore “various options for enhancing corporate value,” while acknowledging that reports were “true” that Tamron had received a “non-binding proposal from Sony Corporation to make [Tamron] a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Sony Group.”

Since that statement on July 30, Tamron has said nothing else.

Sony has likewise been silent since July.

On the same day Tamron acknowledged Sony’s proposal, Sony itself told Reuters that it believed its offer to Tamron would help develop Sony’s imaging business and also enhance Tamron’s corporate value.

Sony was Tamron’s largest outside shareholder, with a 15.35% stake, until Singaporean investment firm Effissimo Capital aggressively increased its stake earlier this year to nearly 17.4%, pushing Sony to second place.

On the topic of Tamron’s corporate value, as Nikkei reported in September, Sony’s acquisition attempt has only helped Tamron so far.

“Investors seem to be pricing in a premium for Tamron shares following Sony’s proposal, counting on it going through,” Nikkei wrote.

That seems to be the smart bet. The fact that neither Tamron nor Sony has said anything about Sony’s acquisition offer since the two companies simultaneously acknowledged its existence, including in either of the two financial reports since, strongly signals that negotiations remain ongoing. If Sony hasn’t pulled out and Tamron hasn’t declined the offer outright by now, it sure seems like wheels are very much in motion.

Sony’s non-binding proposal was reportedly 200 billion yen, or nearly $1.3 billion. Again, Sony already owns over 15% of Tamron, so the 200 billion yen would be for the remaining 85%.

Tamron’s market cap on June 30 was about 224 billion yen. It’s now about 233.7 billion yen. The company’s share price skyrocketed as soon as reports about Sony’s proposal were published, and it hasn’t dropped back down to pre-proposal levels.

For reference, Tamron’s operating profit is up year over year compared to Q3 FY2026, and Sony likewise grew profits in both imaging and image sensor segments compared to the same quarter last year.

hurricane-proof polycarbonate wraps resilient 24-hour public library in miami

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

faced with the challenge of sheltering an automated 24-hour library from hurricanes, the design utilizes an ambiguous, geometric steel frame wrapped entirely in high-velocity polycarbonate paneling.

Julio C. Perez edited by christina vergopoulou November 5, 2026 3 min

Designed by 89th Studio, Le Petite Bibliothèque is a 264-sqft civic shelter sited in North Miami’s Claude Pepper Park, adjacent to the Joe Celestin Recreation Center. Developed to house an automated 24-hour lending library kiosk manufactured by Ohio-based company Envisionware, whose standard modular prototypes had previously failed to meet South Florida’s High Velocity Hurricane Zone (HVHZ) building codes, the project resolves severe wind-load compliance through a bespoke, climate-responsive envelope engineered to withstand Category 5 storms.

The structure by 89th Studio is framed in structural steel with welded and mechanically fastened connections, forming a multi-gabled geometric silhouette that acts as a visual signifier within the park. To avoid the cost and weight of impact-resistant glass, the enclosure utilizes high-velocity-rated polycarbonate panels divided into two functional zones by a horizontal steel stiffener. The placement of this datum is calculated against lowest seasonal sun angles, blocking direct solar glare from the automated interface screens. The upper zone features opaque panels that reduce solar heat gain and double as interchangeable surfaces for public announcements, while the transparent lower perimeter provides direct sightlines into the kiosk to support passive security during unmonitored night hours. Fastened using standard Florida residential storm shutter track hardware to utilize local fabrication labor and reduce costs, the dual-performance skin reverses its function after dark, diffusing interior fixture light outward to establish a luminous profile across the park.

the building’s profile shifts from a series of distinct geometric shapes into a singular cohesive objectmulti-wall polycarbonate paneling replaces conventional, heavy impact glass

architect: 89th Studio  lead designers: Julio Perez, Fausto Rivas

Bitcoin erases post-election gains as Wall Street selloff triggers over $1 billion in crypto liquidations

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The selloff triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets, as total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached $1.09 billion, affecting 211,000 traders.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, and other cryptocurrencies erased their post-election gains on Thursday as a sharp selloff in U.S. technology and AI stocks spilled into cryptocurrency markets, triggering more than $1 billion in leveraged position liquidations over the past 24 hours.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell 6% to $75,700, after touching an intraday low of $75,568, while ether (ETH) dropped more than 7% to $2,247. The declines reversed Wednesday's rally, when bitcoin climbed back above $80,000 following the U.S. midterm elections and signs that the Binance hack had not caused broader market disruption.

Midterm election results appeared to generate short-term optimism, but analysts say they haven't resolved macroeconomic uncertainties.

As of 2 p.m. ET, the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 were down about 3% for the day, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite had dropped 4.7%. The Cboe Volatility Index, or VIX, surged approximately 60%, reflecting heightened investor anxiety.

AI and bitcoin-related mining stocks were among the hardest hit, with Bitdeer and Keel Infrastructure each falling about 20%. American Bitcoin dropped 17% despite announcing a quarterly profit of $126 million. Crypto bellwethers Strategy and Coinbase were each down about 10%.

Of note, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin hit an all-time low after falling more than 20% over the past two days.

The selloff also triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets. About $879.92 million in positions were liquidated over a four-hour period, including $818.90 million in longs and $61.02 million in shorts, according to CoinGlass. Total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached nearly $1.1 billion, affecting nearly 211,000 traders.

It should be noted that liquidation figures are typically based on publicly available data, which may significantly understate the extent of market liquidations.

The reversal suggests Wednesday's rebound may have been driven more by temporary optimism than a meaningful improvement in the market's underlying conditions, according to Wintermute.

The trading firm said the rally reflected a combination of relief following the Binance hack, enthusiasm surrounding the election results, optimism about a potential slowdown in AI spending and falling oil prices. However, those factors proved short-lived as broader macroeconomic pressures persisted.

"It's probably a combination of all of them in some way. What matters is that all of them seemed to be so short-lived that it actually didn't really matter," said Jasper De Maere, an OTC trader at Wintermute. "Trump is still the president for two years, the Middle East is still escalating, inflation is elevated into a strong U.S. economy."

Beyond the immediate market pressures, the election results may also complicate the outlook for crypto regulation, potentially tempering some of the optimism that helped drive Wednesday's rebound.

Paul Howard, senior director at Wincent, said the Democrats' strengthened position in Congress could make bipartisan cooperation necessary for meaningful progress on crypto legislation over the next two years. With the Clarity Act's prospects uncertain, Howard expects the existing regulatory framework to largely remain intact until the next election cycle.

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, said the election could delay crypto legislation but wouldn't fundamentally change longer-term institutional demand for digital assets.

"Investors are still focused on liquidity, interest rates and the broader economy, and those factors will continue to influence the market regardless of which party controls Congress," Kirkley said.

If the weakness continues, Wintermute identified $73,000 and $68,000 as the next levels to watch for bitcoin. The firm said it would need to see bitcoin convincingly reclaim and hold $80,000 before turning more positive.

Meta-backed 250 MW solar farm breaks ground in Arkansas

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Arkansas is getting another 250 megawatts (MW) of solar power, and Meta has signed up to buy it.

EDP Renewables North America (EDPR NA) broke ground on Cypress Knee Solar in Chicot County, in the southeastern corner of the state, on November 4. The solar farm is expected to be completed by 2028, bringing several hundred construction jobs and an estimated $25 million in county revenue over its lifetime.

Meta’s long-term power purchase agreement helps it work toward its pledge to match 100% of its annual electricity use with renewable energy generation. Don’t confuse that pledge with Meta powering its data centers with renewables around the clock, though – Facebook and Instagram’s parent company is also backing new gas plants to meet its growing power demand.

The companies announced the deal in May. Cypress Knee is their third clean-energy agreement, bringing the total capacity covered by Meta’s deals with EDPR NA to 545 MW.

“As energy demand continues to rise, projects like Cypress Knee Solar help strengthen grid reliability while creating jobs, generating meaningful local revenue, and delivering long-term benefits to the communities that host them,” said EDPR NA CEO Sandhya Ganapathy.

EDPR NA says it has already contributed more than $77,000 during project development, including money to restore the Lakeside High School greenhouse, resources for the Lakeside Robotics team, and funding for the Lake Village food pantry.
