Cacio E Pepe Mac &amp; Cheese

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Stovetop mac and cheese is the equivalent of childhood heaven. While I’ll never turn my head at the classic version, this cacio e pepe mac and cheese delivers the same comforting nostalgia, but with a grown-up upgrade. The best part? You most likely already have all of the ingredients on hand. Trust me—once you try this simple, elevated version, you’ll be putting your Kraft days behind you.

The components:All of the ingredients are relatively budget-friendly; the only item you’ll be spending a bit more on is good Pecorino Romano. To make it, you’ll need to toast the black pepper in some butter, then add the condensed milk and whole milk. Once the liquid comes to a simmer, add the American cheese and stir constantly until melted. Off the heat, add the Pecorino and shells and continue to cook for about a minute, just until the sauce tightens around the pasta. Once it coats every noodle perfectly, you’re ready to dig in.

Storage:It’s hard to believe there will be leftovers, but if you’re meal prepping for lunch, store portions in airtight containers in the refrigerator for up to 3 days. Reheat in the microwave or on the stove with a little extra milk to loosen.

Over the past year, I've redeemed 1.3 million IHG One Rewards points for 62 hotel nights. I got 0.88 cents per point on these redemptions, so while TPG's November 2026 valuations peg the value of IHG points at 0.6 cents each, it's certainly possible to get more value when redeeming IHG points.

All these redemptions mean I also have to earn a sizable amount of IHG points each year. There are many ways to earn IHG points, so you have options, whether you need to top off your account or substantially boost your balance. In this article, I'll describe how to earn IHG points.

As you might expect, you can earn IHG points when you stay at participating IHG hotels. However, the exact number of points you'll earn on stays depends on a few factors:

You'll only earn IHG points and get access to elite benefits when you book a qualifying rate through a qualifying method. Qualifying methods include IHG Customer Care, IHG websites, the IHG One Rewards app, directly at the hotel and through authorized travel agents. Authorized travel agents exclude online travel agents and are defined in the IHG terms and conditions as "travel booking platforms operating under direct distribution agreements with IHG which specifically provide for nights booked through their platforms to be qualifying stays."

Related: The best IHG hotels in the world

Your earning rate for qualifying stays will vary by IHG hotel brand. Specifically, you'll earn points as follows:

You'll generally earn points on qualifying room rates and additional purchases charged to your room, including food and beverages. However, at some properties, you may not earn points on other purchases charged to your room.

Except at InterContinental Alliance Resorts, you can earn points on up to nine hotel rooms. Your IHG One Rewards account number must be associated with each room reservation, and you must stay at the property.

Members with IHG elite status earn additional points on stays. As you'd expect, the higher your elite status tier, the more points you'll earn on your stay. Here's how many bonus points IHG elite members will earn:

Here's an example of how these bonus points can add up. If you're a Diamond Elite member staying at a Kimpton hotel for three nights at $200 per night, you'll earn the following:

As a result, you'd take home 12,000 IHG points from your three-night stay — and potentially more if you made any qualifying charges to your room or paid for your stay with a rewards card. Based on TPG's November 2026 valuations, 12,000 IHG One Rewards points are worth $72.

You can often buy up to 5,000 elite qualifying points per night when booking a qualifying paid stay. Look for the option on the "Add Extras" page while booking.

There's no cap on the number of points you can buy each calendar year via these packages. As such, doing so can be a good option if you have already reached the maximum number of points you can outright purchase in a calendar year.

Starting at just 20 nights a year, IHG members earn a choice of Milestone Rewards for every 10 nights (up to 100 nights per year). IHG offers its members various rewards at each milestone, and some may be more valuable than points.

But if you're primarily interested in earning IHG points, you can choose points at each milestone. Specifically, you can earn points through Milestone Rewards each calendar year as follows:

I rarely choose points as Milestone Rewards, as I usually find food-and-beverage rewards, confirmable suite upgrade awards or an annual lounge membership provides more value.

IHG One Rewards frequently offers promotions that let you earn additional points on your hotel stays over the standard earning rates. However, you usually need to register for IHG bonus points promotions before completing your stay.

For IHG promotions that offer multipliers (such as double points), the multiplier typically applies only to the stay's base earnings. So, if you are an IHG elite member, your elite bonus usually won't be multiplied.

Related: How to get more out of every all-inclusive vacation with IHG One Rewards

One of the easiest ways to earn IHG points is by spending on a cobranded IHG credit card. Here's how the four currently available IHG credit cards earn points, including their updated earning categories beginning Jan. 1, 2027 (see the paragraph below the table for some additional earning rates that end Dec. 31):

Through Dec. 31, IHG Premier and Business cardholders will also earn 5 points per dollar spent on all other travel, while no-annual-fee IHG cardholders will also earn 3 points per dollar spent on utilities, internet, cable, phone services and select streaming services.

Cardholders of the Premier, Premier Select and Business cards get automatic access to the valuable IHG fourth-night-free reward perk. But cardholders of the no-annual-fee IHG card will need to spend $5,000 or more with their card within a calendar year to unlock access to the fourth-night-free reward perk through the end of the next calendar year starting in 2027. I've used the fourth-night-free reward perk 13 times during the last year, so it provides a ton of value to me.

Related: The best IHG hotel stays you can book with 200,000 points

You can transfer Chase Ultimate Rewards points and Bilt Points to the IHG One Rewards program. Points transfer at a 1:1 ratio from both programs.

However, I don't recommend transferring points from either program to IHG. Based on TPG's November 2026 valuations, you'd need to redeem IHG points for at least 2.05 cents each to justify transferring Chase points or 2.2 cents each to justify transferring Bilt points.

As such, you'll generally only want to transfer points to IHG if you need to top off your account for a specific award. Otherwise, saving transferable points for higher-value redemptions is generally better.

Related: Why transferable rewards are worth more than airline miles and hotel points

The IHG One Rewards Dine & Earn program allows you to earn points when you dine at participating restaurants and bars. To use the program, you must sign up for an account online and link at least one credit or debit card to your account. Then, you'll automatically earn IHG points when you use a linked card to pay at participating restaurants and bars.

The points you'll earn vary depending on how frequently you use the program and whether you opt in for email notifications. You can earn between 1 and 8 IHG points per dollar spent on eligible purchases. These points are in addition to the rewards earned on your credit card, so consider linking and using one of the best cards for dining purchases.

Related: How to double-, triple- or even quadruple-dip on dining rewards

You can also earn IHG points through the IHG Business Rewards program. This program allows you to earn points when you plan a meeting or event — including a wedding — at select IHG properties.

Once you register for IHG Business Rewards, you can earn 3 points per dollar when you book rooms, meetings and events for others. Qualifying spending includes:

Some properties don't participate in IHG Business Rewards. And you usually can't earn more than 60,000 points on a meeting or event. Plus, qualifying spending usually excludes taxes, gratuities, service fees and the organizer's room.

Another option for earning IHG points is to purchase them. Buying IHG points can be a good deal, especially when the program offers a 100% bonus and you have a high-value redemption in mind.

The standard price to buy IHG points is 1 cent per point if you purchase at least 26,000 points. However, IHG One Rewards offers promotions several times a year during which you can purchase points with up to a 100% bonus. If you buy during a 100% bonus, you can earn IHG points for as little as 0.5 cents each.

TPG values IHG points at 0.6 cents each. Since it's easy for me to get more than 0.5 cents of value per IHG point, I usually consider purchasing IHG points whenever there's a 100% bonus sale. However, you usually can't purchase more than 200,000 IHG points per calendar year.

IHG members can transfer points to another member by paying a $5 fee per 1,000 points transferred. Each member can transfer up to 100,000 points per calendar year and receive up to 500,000 points per calendar year.

IHG members who hold Diamond Elite status — or belong to the IHG Business Rewards program and have "consumed a Business Rewards event in the current or previous calendar year" — can transfer points to other members without paying transfer fees.

So, if you have a colleague or family member who has Diamond Elite status (or is an active member of the IHG Business Rewards program) and wants to transfer IHG points to you, this could be a way to earn IHG points.

Related: Earn even more rewards when dining out with OpenTable

I frequently get significantly more than TPG's valuation of 0.6 cents per IHG point when redeeming for hotel stays, so I find it worthwhile to keep a decent balance. However, I'd generally avoid transferring valuable Chase Ultimate Rewards points or Bilt points to IHG unless you need to top off your account for a specific redemption.

Man fighting to keep badger he nursed back to health after officials reject license

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A Scottish man is fighting to keep a badger named Harriet after saving her from starvation, only for wildlife officials to tell him she must be released into the wild or sent to a zoo.

Graeme Wilson, a 55-year-old ecologist from the Scottish Borders, rescued Harriet in April after the young badger was discovered severely underweight and filthy inside a cattle trough, roughly 300 feet from her sett.

Wilson, who operates a wildlife rescue and rehabilitation service called GW Ecology, took Harriet in and nursed her back to health.

However, he quickly noticed something unusual about the animal. Despite being just a few months old, Harriet appeared remarkably comfortable drinking milk from a bottle, leading him to suspect she had previously been raised by humans.

“It was almost second nature when she saw the bottle,” Wilson told the BBC.

“I have cared for injured badgers before and never have I seen something like it. It was clear then she couldn’t be released back into the wild.”

Wilson reportedly attempted to release Harriet at two different badger setts, but she became extremely distressed and appeared determined to return to him.

Unfortunately, keeping Harriet has now become a legal battle.

According to PEOPLE, Scotland’s wildlife agency, NatureScot, rejected Wilson’s application for an educational license that would allow him to continue caring for Harriet.

Officials instead advised that the badger should be released or transferred to a licensed zoo, something Wilson believes could put her welfare at risk.

Sewer-dwelling man arrested for harassing woman’s children

Bear finally freed by rescuers after getting head stuck in giant stove pipe

Deer put down after attacking man in London park as feeding blamed

“Badgers are companion animals, they are a social animal and unfortunately for Harriet she has decided that humans are her social group,” he explained.

“Can you imagine Harriet approaching someone on a dog walk or in the street? There is only one way that would end.”

He also fears that sending Harriet to a zoo would leave her lonely and distressed.

“Harriet’s upbringing means she needs human interaction, not to be put behind glass and stared at,” Wilson said.

NatureScot has defended its decision, explaining that his application failed to meet the legal requirements for keeping a protected wild animal.

However, the agency clarified that Wilson could allow Harriet to roam freely from his property while continuing to provide food and shelter, provided her movements are not restricted.

The organization says discussions are ongoing to find a solution that prioritizes Harriet’s welfare.

Meanwhile, a GoFundMe campaign has been launched to support her care and potentially fund a specially designed habitat if Wilson receives permission to keep her.

The unusual dispute echoes the controversy surrounding Peanut the squirrel, who was seized from his owner by New York wildlife officials in 2024.

For now, Wilson remains determined to give Harriet a safe future.

“Harriet never asked for any of this — she deserves to live happily,” he said.

Base deploys Denim upgrade on mainnet, replacing Flashblocks with 200ms block times

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Denim retires Flashblocks, adds millisecond-resolution timestamps, and updates B20 transfer and compliance controls.

Base's fourth mainnet upgrade, Denim, is live, with the Coinbase-incubated Layer 2 now producing a full canonical block every 200 milliseconds, or five per second, the project said in a statement shared with The Block on Thursday.

The upgrade also introduces millisecond-resolution timestamps for smart contracts, alongside new transfer protections and compliance controls for tokens using Base's B20 standard.

Base said the upgrade retires Flashblocks and changes how the pending block tag works, making block production "simpler, more consistent, and more reliable." Each block now follows its parent exactly 200 milliseconds later, with the protocol enforcing the interval rather than relying on the sequencer's clock.

Applications that previously relied on Flashblocks must migrate to standard canonical block subscriptions, according to the statement.

"Base’s north star is to bring the world onchain, starting with trading, financing, and payments," the statement read. "Those use cases need a chain that updates state often and lets builders reason about time precisely."

According to the statement, the upgrade also introduces BaseTime, which gives smart contracts access to Unix timestamps in milliseconds. Ethereum-compatible block.timestamp remains unchanged at whole-second resolution, while new RPC fields provide millisecond timestamps for blocks, transactions, and logs.

Denim also introduces reusable compliance policies through a NOT operator and extends transfer-executor restrictions to direct transfers. It also blocks transfers, mints and seizures that send tokens to the token contract's own address, Base said.

The upgrade follows Azul, Beryl and Cobalt, Base's first three independent upgrades since its 2023 launch. Azul introduced a new client stack and multiproof system, while Beryl brought the B20 token standard. Cobalt added conditional transactions and expanded B20 compliance features.

‘Everytime’ Review: Sandra Wollner’s Infinitely Beguiling Portrait of Grief in the Digital Age Is One of the Most Haunting Films of the Decade

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

[Editor’s note: This review was originally published on September 10, 2026 during the 2026 Toronto International Film Festival. 1-2 Special will release the film in limited theaters Friday, November 6, with a wide release to come in February 2027.]

I don’t believe in an afterlife, but that’s OK because the people I love most are never going to die. Not to me. At least, not in the way they used to before our species began to migrate online, to store our memories in the cloud, and to digitally encode the raw data of our existence onto everything we touch.

My father was born in 1939, the same year that Iowa State College scientists John Vincent Atanasoff and Clifford Berry built the prototype for the first binary-based computer; in the months after he died 75 years later, I often felt the reflex to reach down and call him from the iPhone that fit inside my pocket. To ask if he’d seen the Rangers game, or to tell him that I’d gotten engaged. I knew that he was gone, of course, but the piece of consumer technology I’d used to connect with him when he was alive had created a separate neural pathway between my brain and his being — a crude one-way network that didn’t begin to break down until much later in my grieving process.  Related Stories I Remember ‘Rocky’: My Front Row Seat to the Marketing of a Million-to-One Blockbuster MUBI Has Cleared the Path for Sandra Hüller to Land Multiple Oscar Nominations This Season

A decade later, those pathways have grown exponentially more complicated and convincing. The emergence of AI has led to a variety of ghoulish attempts at deathlessness and resurrection (from bots that feed on your grandma’s Facebook data to the ChatGPT Theodore Roosevelt who Trump was “honored to be with today” at the former’s Presidential Library), but such products and publicity stunts miss the fact that everyone is already being (temporarily) immortalized on a server farm somewhere, the traces of our existence shed into the digital ether like a Pollock-like splotch of invisible ink.

Charlie Kirk’s face is Twitter’s most ubiquitous meme. My long-deceased college friend you’ve never heard of sends me hacked emails full of links to “old pictures they found of us together.” The cancer-stricken hedgehog I fostered for a few months in 2016 still cycles onto my home screen at random sometimes, and he’s now been buried behind a house in suburban New Jersey for longer than he was ever alive. People have always looked for errant glimmers of the dead in the world around them, but now the past runs parallel to — imprints upon, and circles around — the present to a degree that can severely confuse the relationship between the two.

Sandra Wollner doesn’t believe in an afterlife either, but she’s certainly attuned to the mysteries of this one. Picking up where the equally spectral likes of “Personal Shopper” and “Aftersun” left up, the Austrian writer/director’s mystical and magnificently beguiling “Everytime” takes a rather ambivalent stance towards what happens when people die these days, or at least — per its curious title — towards how we mourn them from inside the flat circle of a temporal overlap where yesterday and tomorrow are folded into the pockets of an ever-expanding now.

There are no clear answers to be found in this astonishing third feature, which shares a number of thematic similarities with Wollner’s “The Trouble with Being Born,” but retains one crucial difference (that film, about a man who has an implied sexual relationship with a child-sized android coded with the memories of his missing 10-year-old daughter, unsurprisingly had trouble securing American distribution). Indeed, the grounded portraiture of the movie’s first 70 minutes eventually gives way to a final act so cosmic and surreal that it becomes all but impossible to pinpoint the precise location of Wollner’s characters in space-time; to say for certain whether “Everytime” has glitched into another dimension, or if it has invited another dimension to glitch into our own. Initially timeless in its nuanced and deeply recognizable depiction of loss, and then even more so as it gets devoured by the meta-modern flourishes that have fringed its story from the start, this is perhaps the most forward-thinking film I’ve ever seen about the changing face of grief.

The premise couldn’t be simpler, though Wollner’s incredible prologue — a careful arrangement of low-event long takes that emphasize how people move through spaces in time — trains our eyes to privilege texture over incident. While there’s little to distinguish single mother Ella (“Everyone Else” star Brigit Minichmayr) and her children from any of the other families strolling through the soft orange haze of a summer afternoon in Berlin, our senses pick up on the uneasy tension between the watchfulness of Gregory Oke’s 35mm camera work and the vivid intensity of the film’s sound design (it’s worth noting that Oke also shot “Aftersun”). The voices are close, but the bodies far away. Teenage Jessie (Carla Hüttermann) turns her head to look at an unseen pop as her family walks home through their local metro station, but we don’t think much of it at the moment. It’s probably nothing. And yet, I suppose it could be anything.

At the very least, that sharp noise is enough to suggest that the film’s environments may not be as benign as they seem, and a silent but soon unbearable tension starts to mount as the day wears on. As Jessie’s boyfriend Lux (Tristán López) comes over to hang out while her kid sister Melli (Lotte Shirin Keiling) annoys the shit out of her by banging on a keyboard; as Ella cooks dinner; as Jessie plays “Minecraft” late into the night on an island bathed in the light of a sinking square sun.

Later, in the waning hours before Jessie’s family is due to leave for a holiday in Tenerife (where she once vacationed as a toddler), Jessie and Lux will sneak out to a dance club, take a few pills, and watch the next day begin from their perch atop the roof of an apartment building. He will note that he’s recently noticed a strange number of seagulls in Berlin, and then fall asleep. She will stand up to film one of them flying past on her iPhone, and then — in one of the most arresting screen images in recent memory, a vertiginous collision of everything and nothing all at once — she will fall off the edge of the building to her death. The city doesn’t notice. The camera scans the treetops. The euphoric synth-pop of David Schweighart’s score kicks in as we suddenly begin to soar through the skies of Jessie’s “Minecraft” world, eventually plunging down through the dirt and into the unrendered levels of code below.

From there, “Everytime” settles into a crushingly quotidian register. Months have passed, and everyone is doing what they can to move on with their lives. Ella takes work calls while visiting her daughter’s grave, while the stoic young Melli texts life updates and other messages to her dead sister’s phone (she’s big on emojis). But things are not as settled as they seem, and Lux’s return from a semester abroad forces Ella and Melli into a more direct confrontation with Jessie’s memory — with the unresolved feelings she left behind. The girls keep running into Lux (and to his nice new girlfriend) around town, and soon begin spending time with him on purpose. They are bound together not only by their shared grief, but also by the unspoken guilt and blame that Lux and Ella can only hope to expiate through each other.

While Jessie is seldom mentioned by name, the bizarre mutual reliance between a middle-aged woman and her dead teenage daughter’s former boyfriend makes her the subject of every scene (much to the annoyance of people who would prefer to compartmentalize the pain of her loss). Jessie is the reason why Ella and Melli attend Lux’s birthday party; Jessie is the reason why Lux joins Ella for a tense lunch at her ex-husband’s house, and why he begins sleeping over at her apartment.

But these domestic frictions are — largely, but not merely — a means to an end so far as Wollner is concerned, as her film leverages them to focus on a series of increasingly abstract phenomena. Jessie is certainly the reason why each of the locations we saw her walk through before her death feels haunted when “Everytime” revisits them, but could she also be the reason why the shadow that falls across her bedroom at night appears to resemble a polygonal cube? Why a seemingly pilotless drone shows up to beckon Ella into a pitch-black parking garage? Why the dead girl’s computer glows with an unnatural brightness whenever the lights turn off?

Wollner’s characters never ask such questions aloud, but she poses them to all three of her leads in a way that compels them to look for signs in the digital detritus that Jessie left behind. Lux is drawn back to the old pictures and voice notes on his phone, while Mille stares at the WhatsApp chat with her sister as if waiting for a response. These signs complement, but never overwhelm, Wollner’s attention to the analog poetry of the natural world — to the shimmer along the surface of a lake, or the sound of the wind rustling through the trees in the woods where Jessie and Lux got high on her last night.

It’s easy to imagine a version of this film that more explicitly addressed how digital technology is transforming how we mourn the dead, but Wollner would rather plumb the mysteries of the present than speculate about the answers of the future; the portals between worlds have already been ripped open, and will only begin to lose their power as they get labeled and branded.

Eventually, Ella makes the spontaneous decision to bring Mille and Lux to Tenerife for the trip they never got to take. That’s where things get weird, and the math of the universe starts to contradict itself. It would be a terrible disservice to reveal anything specific about what happens next (and so I won’t), but the extraordinary magic trick that “Everytime” pulls off in its final stretch is rooted less in incident than in execution.

Its ineffable power belongs to how matter-of-factly Wollner tears apart the fabric of her film’s reality at the same time as she threads it together, and how the look on Minichmayr’s slacken face — her restrained but masterful performance a wonder of tightly bottled pain and lingering disbelief — reconciles those opposite energies with the instinctive naturalness of a mother’s love. It also belongs to the almost unquestioning acceptance that Keiling endows within Melli, the young actor’s posture embodying how this movie teeters on the precipice between a child-like vision of the world (soft and malleable) and the more hardened sense of reality that arrives with adulthood.

Wollner may not trace the character’s arc in the legible terms of a typical coming-of-age story, but Melli experiences a remarkable transformation over the course of the film, her petulant first line (“You always leave your stuff lying around!”) assuming an unforeseeable new context by the time we arrive at her closing narration.

As a medium born from — and once dependent upon smoothing over — the friction between stillness and movement, stasis and change, cinema has always been uniquely adept at conveying the radical effect that technology can exert on how we perceive time and space in the world around us. The switch from celluloid to computer chips saw the movies transform from an illusory art form to a representative one, at the great and continued risk of forfeiting its original power. Engaging in a thrilling and increasingly contested negotiation between 35mm film stock and crude DV video formats, “Everytime” pulls at that tug-of-war from both ends until the uncertain space at the center grows large enough to sail through, and still enough to hang like a painting in the sky.

I am now a father myself, the parent of two young kids whose faces sometimes remind me of my own dad in the same tactile way that people have been survived by their children since before the invention of the wheel. We keep a framed 4×6 photograph of him with a cigar in his mouth on the Blu-ray shelf in our living room, and I sometimes catch my kids talking about it as if the picture itself were their grandfather, rather than just a capture of his likeness. Ask my six-year-old to draw a self-portrait and he’ll sketch you the image of his Roblox avatar. If I ever were to lose him, I wonder how long it would take my nervous system to process that he was gone.

Like many 21st century parents, my wife and I take photos and videos of our kids throughout the day without thinking, and — after wrestling them to bed at night in an exasperating forever war of attrition — we invariably scroll through our phones and take stock of their cuteness in peace. Stills, videos, stickers, and more, these two-dimensional images, I sometimes feel with an uncertain pang of dismay, are our real children. The versions of them we commit to memory. The after images we see in our dreams. They are the kids we mourn as our son and daughter eagerly outgrow their current models every time you look away for too long, and the ones we would cling to in the unthinkable event that either of them disappeared from this mortal coil before we did.

And so, Wollner’s staggering new film suggests with a tender but uneasy twinkle in its eye, we’ve gradually steeped ourselves in a world that guarantees they never will. How strange and unnatural is that? Compared to the alternative, I fear it may not feel that strange or unnatural at all.

“Everytime” is screening at the 2026 Toronto International Film Festival. 1-2 Special will release it in theaters in the United States.

Opinion: African midfielder is destined to join Liverpool, he could be the next Nyoni

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

An exciting Malian midfielder is destined to join Liverpool.

When you think of Mali and the Reds, you remember Djimi Traore. He might not have been one of the best players to have ever played for the club, but his contributions in Istanbul in 2005 on that fated May evening will be remembered forever.

Traore's blocks and the way he gave it his all to help inspire the greatest comeback in the history of the sport are indelible. They live on through the stories and moving images that have been recounted time and time again about that night.

Another Malian. This time a midfielder who was so good when he first arrived. At his best, he looked like he was going to be on par with the likes of Michael Essien.

But injuries hampered Sissoko's time at Anfield.

Liverpool need a midfielder this winterThe Reds could so desperately do with someone of Sissoko's profile in this winter market. What the Malian was known for was his brilliance defensively and his composure on the ball. Right now, Andoni Iraola's squad is lacking a lot of players capable of instilling that composure on the ball. Dominik Szoboszlai and Ryan Gravenberch are both erratic midfielders. Alexis Mac Allister has stepped into their shoes and has done really well. Trey Nyoni as a mere teenager, has right now also become pretty much the first name on the teamsheet when it comes to the midfield. Iraola clearly needs more in that department. He needs players who are capable of playing like Nyoni and progressing the ball from deep.

Its cuffing season — here are the best dating apps for serious relationships

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Finding love isn't for the weak. Dating app exhaustion is realer than ever, and while people want to branch out and meet in person, they're finding it difficult.

One reason is that dating apps are a legitimate way to find a partner, not just a short-term one. Nearly 44 percent of adults say they use dating apps to find a long-term partner rather than just casual dating or hookups, according to the Pew Research Center. One in 10 partnered adults met their current partner through a dating app, and for younger adults and LGBTQ people, that number rises.

Dating apps aren't going away, despite fatigue. And it is possible to make them work for you if you want to find your special someone. The best hookup apps, reviewed by our experts AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections Tinder — top pick for fast hookups Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups Pure — best for anonymous hookups OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

Even using the "right" app for you may still bring moments of uncertainty and frustration (you're dating, after all), but finding the app with features that most closely align with your dating style and the type of partnership you're looking for can still make online dating worth it.

If you've been using apps for a while and are experiencing serious burnout, it's always fair to take some time off and come back to them when you feel ready. Our guide will be here for you! Even the best app can feel exceptionally tedious when you simply don't have the energy.

There are so many dating apps, but not all of them are created equal when it comes to finding a serious, committed relationship.

Some free dating apps are better suited for casual flings or hookups (e.g., Tinder, Grindr, etc.), while others have matching algorithms and profile features specifically designed to help users find meaningful connections (e.g., eharmony, OkCupid, Hinge, and Coffee Meets Bagel).

The good news is that we've done the research (and hands-on testing) to figure out which apps work best for long-term relationships, and Match Group isn't the only player in the game. Plus, all of these platforms are available via Google Play and the App Store, so having an Android or iPhone won't limit your options. Some old-school dating sites still maintain desktop versions.

To find your match, here are the best dating apps for serious relationships in 2026:

Match is a popular dating site and app for people in their 30s and 40s looking for a serious relationship. According to the Match website, 48.6 percent of members are between the ages of 30 and 49, which means this dating pool is filled with adults who are ready for a committed, meaningful relationship.

So, if you're not quite ready for the seriousness of eharmony or want a more inclusive dating experience, Match is a good alternative. It's a platform designed for people who are ready to take their dating journey seriously but without the immediate expectation of marriage.

Match (the OG app of Match Group) has become a popular dating app for singles looking for long-term relationships. With 25+ years of experience in the dating industry, it's no surprise this app comes out on top in most of our dating app guides.

Match refuses to be left behind, constantly evolving its platform to stay current for all ages. Its questionnaire focuses on shared interests and important lifestyle factors like whether you want children or have pets and dealbreakers such as smoking or drinking, all so you can find a compatible partner. It's also slightly less expensive than eharmony, making it more accessible to try out for a month or two.

In June 2024, Match partnered with relationship and dating expert Jay Shetty (now Match's Relationship Advisor) to tackle "disillusionment" in the dating world. The app now offers features like the "Date Differently Challenge," which is basically an in-depth quiz that helps users figure out what their main dating obstacle is and how to overcome it. A new "Core Values" section also gives members a chance to highlight their top three values to help them find their perfect match. Examples include friendship, humor, ambition, and optimism.

Read our full review of AdultFriendFinder.

If you're ready to say "I do," then eharmony, one of the best dating sites for marriage-minded singles, could be your ideal matchmaker. Known for its focus on compatibility and long-term commitment, eharmony appeals to traditional and largely heterosexual online daters — it's basically the Charlotte York of dating apps.

Unlike swipe-based apps, eharmony prioritizes compatibility by using a comprehensive questionnaire to match you with users with similar values and interests. While not everyone wants to tie the knot, we chose eharmony as one of the best dating apps for serious relationships because of its commitment to finding compatible partners and its long history of success stories.

A spokesperson for the site says it's been used by 54 million people and is responsible for 4 percent of U.S. marriages. While we haven't independently verified these numbers, we can say that eharmony has built up a reputation for being the dating site to go on if you want the white-picket-fence fantasy in the very near future. We just wish it didn't cost so much — and that you could message users without having to pay those high prices. But if you're investing in something serious, this site might be worth it for you.

Although eharmony's reputation also involves being known as a more traditional (i.e. conservative) dating site, it recently expanded its inclusivity and now welcomes users of all sexual orientations and gender identities. The app has become so welcoming that the company is in hot water with some conservative religious groups who claim it has abandoned its traditional values. One Million Moms even started a petition to have LGBTQ+ commercials removed from eharmony's advertising.

Hinge is an algorithm-oriented dating app where instant gratification meets genuine connection. If you're craving a dating app experience that feels more intentional, Hinge could be your answer.

Think of it as a blend of Tinder and Match, focusing on depth rather than surface-level attraction. With detailed profiles and multiple ways to interact, Hinge helps you find someone who appreciates the real you.

Young people looking to at least go on a few dates with the same person before staying the night was a major blind spot for dating sites — until Hinge blew up. The general appeal might be in the same realm as Tinder, but Hinge's unique profile criteria and algorithms based on those criteria set the scene for long-term potential. By covering so many bases before the first message, Hinge lowers the chance of meeting someone you'd hate.

The app's motto, "designed to be deleted," reflects its commitment to helping users find genuine connections and potentially find someone worth leaving the app for. Hinge's real-world statistics speak for themselves, with many users reporting great first dates and expressing interest in second dates. Money talks, too: Hinge continually has increased revenue and paid users, according to the last several earnings calls. The same can't be said for Tinder.

In June 2026, Hinge launched Signals, a purple heartbeat on one's profile telling others that they exhibit good dating behavior. Some requirements include having an account for over a week, and being in good standing (aka following Community Guidelines). But if users want the coveted badge, they also need to act on it, doing things such as responding to people and actually scheduling dates. It goes along with Hinge's philosophy of encouraging thoughtful participation.

If you're fed up with the dated gender norms that plague most dating apps, Bumble may be your best bet. Bumble flips the script, putting the power in the hands of women and non-binary individuals to initiate the first conversation.

By giving the reins to this empowered group, Bumble creates a space where women and non-binary folks can feel more in control of their dating experience and where unsolicited creepiness takes a back seat.

Bumble stands out as a refreshing alternative to the chaotic swiping culture in the world of dating apps. The 72-hour match expiration encourages timely interaction, a plus for anyone focused on serious relationships. Bumble's free communication features (text, voice, video) also let you get to know a match without investing in premium options.

We've all encountered serial swipers, mindlessly liking every profile without genuine intentions. Bumble's unique approach, with its rule that women and non-binary individuals must initiate the conversation, effectively combats this behavior and encourages more meaningful connections.

In 2024, Bumble introduced Opening Moves, a way that men can technically message first in heterosexual connections, but that seems to have led to mixed results. In 2026, Bumble quietly removed Opening Moves in Mexico and Australia — and later in the year changed the rules for everyone. Now, men can message first too.

This year, Bumble has also gotten some flak. Its founder and CEO, Whitney Wolfe Herd, announced that the company will kill the swipe feature by the end of 2026. She wasn't specific about what will replace it, but she has discussed a new, AI-powered Bumble. The move has received some criticism, but Bumble is also hosting more IRL events. So, it remains to be seen what the Bumble experience will be like in the next few months.

OkCupid is designed for individuals who prioritize social justice, equality, and progressive ideals in their search for love and meaningful connections. With robust profile customization options, users can proudly display their political affiliations, causes they support, and social issues they're passionate about right on their dating profile.

Young, progressive singles are more likely to believe that politics aren't just a side topic – they're the foundation of a strong relationship. OkCupid gets it. Their 2017 redesign wasn't just about millennial aesthetics (though we're pretty impressed with the new interface). It also helps you dodge dates with people whose views make you cringe. With tons of options for gender identities, sexual orientations, and pronouns, OkCupid's also an inclusive space for non-binary and queer folks to find love and be themselves.

Users can weed out people they'd fight with on Facebook by answering inquiries like "Would you date someone who keeps a gun in the house?" or "Should the government require children to be vaccinated for preventable diseases?"

As of 2023, OkCupid is also tapping into the powers of generative AI to improve its matchmaking questions.

“OkCupid matches people on what matters to them through in-app matching questions," Michael Kaye, Director of Brand Marketing and Communications at OkCupid, said in a press release. "At the beginning of last year, we decided to have ChatGPT write our matching questions for us, and the chatbot-generated questions have been answered almost 4 million times."

If you're crushing big goals in your career and personal life but swiping through endless profiles leaves you feeling, well, "meh," then EliteSingles might offer the type of dating environment you've been waiting for.

Think of it as the Soho House of dating apps without the cost or waitlist of Raya. As a warning, this site can show profiles from outside your search radius, and definitely has a smaller user base than other sites on this list, but it can be worth checking out if finding someone career-oriented is a top priority for you when dating.

For those unwilling to budge when it comes to their partner's educational values and passion for success, EliteSingles attempts to create a space where you can find a range of people who you know off the bat share similar values when it comes to your professional life. The site's claim to fame is that 82 percent of members are college grads (most between 33 and 50).

This is the place for folks who have hit a comfortable stride professionally and are now pursuing a partner who's at the same point in their life. The site's questionnaire decodes 29 personality traits to suggest matches who not only have an income compatible with yours but with whom you'd mesh based on communication styles, ideal first dates, and whether you're one to make the first move.

Past users report a feed full of people living far away than they indicated in their preferences. It's borderline ironic, considering the whole conceit of the site is about showing you a specific pool of daters. Plus, the lowest distance you can filter your search to is 50 miles away, so there's built-in leeway for the reality that looking for a more specific type of person may require you to expand your physical location horizons some. And unfortunately, it's hard for any dating website to catch anyone lying about their job or income.

HER isn't just another dating app; it's a social hub and dating platform crafted exclusively for lesbians, bisexual women, queer women, and non-binary individuals within the LGBTQIA2S+ community.

If you've ever felt drained by mainstream options or encountered creepy predators, HER is the welcoming community you've been waiting for. It provides an inclusive space where you can be your truest self and connect with other LGBTQIA2S+ people who share similar interests.

Apps like Tinder and Bumble are technically for *everyone,* but they'll be damned if they don't sneak some male profiles into feeds belonging to people who requested otherwise. Given the existence of Grindr, the need for an online dating arena specifically for queer women was clear — thus, HER. Even among the best dating platforms for women, HER is a standout.

The founders of the award-winning app are committed to cultivating a space that’s “so ragingly queer" that frustrated women can delete apps that don't feel like home. As the user base of over 4 million grows, HER could widen your dating pool beyond the queer women you already know. As the app grows, users online have complained that there does seem to be an increase in bot accounts, so if you do come across a profile that seems suspiciously sparse, you'll want to remain wary.

Match Group recently bought HER, which caused a mixed reaction from users, but the app remains a popular dating app for queer women, by queer women. The app recently introduced a feature like Instagram stories called "Feelings," so users can tell each other real-time updates of their mood and what they're looking for.

Plenty of Fish (POF) is an active online dating platform home to a diverse pool of singles searching for love, meaningful connections, and more. While it may not be tailored exclusively for serious relationships, its large user base provides a sea of possibilities for individuals willing to cast their net a bit wider in the quest for their ideal catch.

POF has made a splash in online dating, not just because of its quirky name. With a proven track record of successful matches, POF has garnered a loyal following and has become a platform for many singles looking for love. (A close friend of mine found her husband on POF, and their journey from first message to "I do" is a testament to the potential for genuine connections found on the platform.)

What sets POF apart from other dating apps is its massive user base, which means you have an extensive pool of potential matches to explore. This diversity opens up endless possibilities, ensuring you can find someone who aligns with your dating preferences and relationship goals.

One caveat to this is that Plenty of Fish won't strictly stick to your distance preferences when showing matches, so you might find yourself in a situation where the person you're really connecting with isn't exactly close by. If that's a deal breaker for you, you may want to try out another app.

Once you connect with someone who catches your eye, the chat and messaging features make it easy to spark a conversation and build a real connection. Think flirtatious banter, meaningful messages, and endless possibilities to get to know someone deeper.

Feeling frustrated with dating apps designed for a much younger demographic? Not only is SilverSingles one of the best dating sites for finding serious relationships (an app version is also available), but it's also designed specifically for mature singles looking for love. This platform is here to swipe you right into a fulfilling relationship.

SilverSingles understands mature adults' unique needs and desires. Focusing on compatibility and shared values, this dating app is designed to help you find a potential partner who's at a similar stage in life, ensuring a higher likelihood of sparking a long-term, meaningful connection.

Dating over 50 means that, more than likely, this is your second or third attempt at love. When match or eharmony's user bases still pose too much of a never-married, no-kids crowd, SilverSingles is here to let you know that putting yourself back out there during your golden years doesn't have to be scary.

A sister site to EliteSingles, SilverSingles uses the same intense compatibility system that EliteSingles does, bringing in the popular big five personality traits to assess five levels of your being: openness to experience, conscientiousness, extraversion, agreeableness, and neuroticism. Rather than overwhelming users with an endless sea of profiles, SilverSingles presents a curated selection of five handpicked matches per day, saving time and energy. Just be sure to pay attention to the locations of potential matches, though (like its sister site, SilverSingles has a habit of ignoring location radius preferences).

Coffee Meets Bagel is designed for singles who appreciate a more curated and personalized experience. Instead of overwhelming you with hundreds of options, Coffee Meets Bagel delivers a limited number of highly compatible matches, or "bagels," daily. It's kind of like having a personal assistant but for dating.

Every day, CMB offers seven of its best matches (bagels) for you, chosen by your answers to prompts, how you swiped on yesterday's batch, and who have already expressed interest in you. This slow and steady approach is great for singles who are rusty, introverted, or don't feel like scrolling every free minute.

You're encouraged to get the ball rolling with matches disappearing in seven days. It's less pressure than Bumble's 24-hour time limit but still eliminates a list of people who don't care enough to respond or plan a meet-up. Like a few other apps, CMB won't show your profile to other users unless you have a clear picture, automatically filtering the whole section of faceless profiles you'll find on other apps.

Another standout feature of CMB is the activity report at the bottom of everyone's profile. Though only available to premium users, it'll show a user's stats, like how often they're active and how long it takes them to reply.

We know, we know — Tinder is a hookup app. But for what it's worth, one of our own editors found his long-time partner on Tinder. If you want to match with as many people as possible as you search for your next relationship, Tinder's user base is unmatched.

Tinder, the iconic dating app that ignited swipe culture, is the go-to platform for casual dating, fun encounters, and hookups. While you may not find someone looking to settle down, Tinder isn't a bad place to be if you don't mind dating around or taking things slowly at first. Plus, with the app's new "Relationship Goals" feature, it's easier to see whether or not a potential match would be interested in something long-term.

Let's just get this one out of the way. If you're thinking of dating apps, you're thinking of Tinder. This app pioneered the now-ubiquitous swiping function, revolutionizing the world of online dating and boasting 1.6 billion swipes per day. What started out as a niche hookup app has turned into a wingman that most of us have used at least once. As you're served a series of photos, swipe right if you like what you see and left if you don't. When you both indicate interest, it's a match.

Sure, you may not find someone looking to settle down for a while, but if you don't mind dating around or taking things slow at first, Tinder isn't a bad place to be. Plus, with the "Relationship Goals" feature, it's easier to suss out from a quick glance at someone's profile whether or not they'd be interested in something long-term or if they're just looking for a casual relationship.

Both Bumble and Hinge have success story campaigns. Both apps are known for being more relationship-focused than hookup-focused. The difference will come down to which one you prefer using.

Bumble began as the app where "women make the first move" (message first in heterosexual matches), but in 2026 they changed the rules, so now men can message first, too. (It was also recently acquired and is going private five years after its IPO. We'll monitor how Bumble changes due to the sale.)

Hinge, meanwhile, is known for its prompts, which encourage users to ask questions or start conversations. Hinge also introduced Your Turn Limits, a cap on how many unanswered messages you can have in your queue, last year. If you have eight people to respond to, Hinge now limits your ability to match with someone new (you either have to respond or unmatch). This hints at a "quality over quantity" approach to dating.

There are numerous great dating apps that cater to different use cases. Overall, we believe that Hinge is the best one for most people looking for a long-term relationship, as it boasts a large user base, a proven track record of creating successful relationships (as evidenced by The Knot survey!), and strikes the sweet spot between casual and serious relationships.

Some folks, though, may find that Match and eharmony, and the more built-out profile structure and mature user base of both, fit their needs better. And people still find beaus on plenty of other apps, like Tinder and Bumble.

The above apps won't automatically put you on a path to marriage, but they will improve your odds, especially if you do a bit of trial and error with the different apps we've recommended.

As with most dating topics, this question doesn't have an easy, straightforward answer. The fact is, with large user bases come a variety of types of people, just like when you're dating without apps.

However, we've tested each of the apps on our list to confirm that there are users with verified profiles that have been thoroughly filled out, giving the impression that they're actually looking for love. Our biggest recommendations? See which app you feel like you fit into best, and if something feels weird or spammy, it probably is.

If you're more interested in flings than a lifelong partner, you can try our recommendations of the best apps for hookups. After all, Grindr users can be genuine too, albeit in a hornier way.

We can't state with any certainty which app has the highest success, especially since the apps make wildly different claims about their success rates. In creating this guide, we consulted the limited data that is available to bring you the most reliable dating apps, but when it comes to dating apps, a lot of evidence is anecdotal.

Eharmony claimed to be responsible for four percent of U.S. marriages back in 2015, which is a great statistic. But it's tough to verify — and that was ten years ago. More recently, a 2024 survey from The Knot of nearly 8,000 recently-engaged couples found that of those who met on a dating app (27 percent), most met on Hinge, 36 percent. A quarter (25 percent) met on Tinder, and 20 percent on Bumble.

So, you have options! Despite Tinder's hookup reputation, for instance (which is a perception that the current head of Tinder, Spencer Rascoff, wants it to shed), daters still found the loves of their lives swiping there.

Our approach for testing dating apps goes beyond reading app descriptions, Reddit complaints, and user reviews. We believe that the best way to judge a dating app is to actually use it. That's why our reviews, written by our Sex & Relationships team, are based on real-world experiences. We share our honest opinions, the pros and cons, and an overall assessment of each platform, so you can make an informed decision about where to find your perfect match.

We've been swiping, matching, and messaging on many of these apps, like eharmony, Match, OkCupid, and Elite Singles, for years, so we know firsthand what works and what doesn't. We also looked into each app's usability, cost, safety and privacy measures, and commitment to diversity and inclusion.

Their Kids Loved The Little Gym. Then They Saw a $20 Million Franchise Opportunity.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Griffin Gordon and Kevin Condon were The Little Gym customers before they became franchise investors. Now they own 30 gyms.

Before Griffin Gordon and Kevin Condon owned dozens of The Little Gym locations, they were parents watching their children enjoy gymnastics classes at the franchise. Their kids were tumbling, running and leaving class asking to do it all again.

As a customer, Gordon was impressed that The Little Gym staff members knew the children’s names, greeted their parents and made families feel welcome.

Gordon and Condon had met years earlier at the University of Chicago’s Booth School of Business before pursuing different careers. Condon became a co-founder of Hidden River Strategic Capital, a Philadelphia-based private equity firm that invests in small businesses. Gordon split his career between private equity investing and running small and midsize companies, later founding an investment firm focused on franchisees.

Their paths converged around an opportunity involving Richard Moore, a longtime Little Gym operator whose family had spent nearly three decades building the business. Moore wanted to keep growing. His parents were ready to retire. An advisor introduced him to Gordon and Condon, who saw a chance to pair an experienced operator with the capital and support to expand.

In 2023, Gordon and Condon’s firms invested in seven North Carolina locations of The Little Gym, forming the foundation of a business called Somersault. Three years later, the partners say their footprint has grown to more than 30 locations, with another 10-plus in development. Their business generates nearly $20 million in revenue.

They credit that expansion to a combination of familiar ingredients, including an established brand. Here’s why they decided to invest in The Little Gym and open new franchise locations.

The following interview has been lightly edited for clarity and concision.

Why invest so aggressively, growing from seven locations to more than 30?Kevin Condon: Ultimately, we’re backing management teams and organizational leaders. We have a really good team here, which has allowed us to scale at this pace and given us the conviction to continue putting money behind the business.

The other piece is the repeatable process in franchising. You acquire a business or open a location, and you have a playbook. With a good team and good operators, you can repeat that playbook. We’ve taken a strong brand and expanded through acquisitions and new openings.

Griffin Gordon: Kevin’s firm has done a lot of franchisee investing. My firm focuses solely on it. The Little Gym has the things we find attractive: a growing market of parents who want high-quality services for their kids, a strong brand and attractive unit economics when the business is operated well.

What else made this particular opportunity attractive?Gordon: In our view, The Little Gym is the best brand serving its target age range. There are imitators, but we don’t think they match the quality of its curriculum and operating playbook.

We also have a strong franchisor partner in Unleashed Brands. To invest in franchisees, you need a partnership approach and confidence that the franchisor values that relationship, too.

How did those factors turn into an actual investment?Gordon: It was equal parts purposeful and opportunistic. We were interested in franchisee investing because of its repeatability and scalability. We were also focused on child enrichment because we thought it was an attractive market if you did things right.

How much did being customers influence your decision to start locations of your own?Condon: We only have so much time and money to allocate. The Little Gym offered the opportunity to invest in what we consider a category leader in youth enrichment, particularly children’s recreational gymnastics and related activities.

The system has been around for 50 years. This year was its 50th anniversary, which was a major milestone for the franchise.

There’s an established playbook, and both our business and the overall system are growing. When you have a strong brand and the unit economics to open a location, ramp it up and become profitable, you can create returns for investors, jobs for employees and outcomes for families. That makes it easy to understand why we want to continue investing our time and money.

What are the most important elements of that repeatable playbook?Gordon: There are two sides to it: the fundamentals that don’t change, and the opportunity to modernize the business for today’s parents and children. The fundamentals include building and investing in a great team, genuinely caring about the curriculum and developing authentic connections with the communities where you operate.

How do those fundamentals connect with changes to the business?Gordon: You also have to work with the franchisor to make sure the curriculum supports the outcomes everyone wants: Confident, self-assured, well-adjusted children who have an opportunity to become physically active early in life.

That dovetails with Unleashed Brands’ investments in infrastructure. Before our investment, The Little Gym didn’t have much in the way of technology, systems and infrastructure. It operated more like a small-business-owned enterprise.

Since Unleashed Brands’ acquisition, we’ve been able to combine the traditional strengths of the business with improvements in the technology we use to operate it and the systems customers interact with.

There’s also been a more thoughtful approach to customer acquisition and marketing, alongside continued investment in the programming. The offering has to remain competitive with parents’ changing demands and schedules.

Condon: The core curriculum is the secret sauce. It’s been part of what has made The Little Gym special for 50 years. But you can build on that. For example, Unleashed introduced an app this year to help parents engage with their children’s classes and see milestones and pictures.

That’s something that obviously didn’t exist 50 years ago. It connects what The Little Gym has historically done well with what a modern parent wants from a class.

Both of you have children who attended. What were their experiences like?Condon: Smiles from ear to ear. They’d spend 45 to 60 minutes in class, come out sweaty and exhausted, and their first question would be, “Can we do that again?”

There’s a joy in watching children move and take appropriate risks, whether they’re learning a cartwheel or somersault, jumping, running, singing, following directions or engaging with other kids.

Much of what I’m describing is in that 2-to-6-year-old age range, although we serve younger and older children, too. I saw it with my own kids, and we see it across the children coming through our locations. There’s a consistency to that experience. It’s unadulterated childhood joy.

Why franchise instead of starting an independent business?Condon: In small business, the opportunity to be part of something with a name as established as The Little Gym is attractive. We invest in both franchised and non-franchised businesses. One benefit of franchising is that you can be part of something much bigger than you could create on your own.

If we started a competitor, we wouldn’t have 50 years of history or an established brand. Franchising gives us an affiliation with a much larger organization and system.

You pay royalties and operate within certain rules. But in this case, we believe the playbook and brand provide more benefits than going it alone.

Gordon: When done correctly, franchising should be like buying a small business with the benefit of a national platform. Those two things should work together. That’s what we believe we have with The Little Gym.

What should someone do if they’re interested in franchising but don’t know where to start?Gordon: One of the best things about franchising is the availability of information. Not every detail is public, but the information you need to understand the model is there.

In particular, there’s a list of franchisees in the back of the franchise disclosure document. If you’re evaluating a system, start calling owners and learning from them. These are people who have already done what you’re trying to do. I can’t think of a better resource.

Condon: Find something you’re passionate about, particularly if you’ll be the operator. Even if you won’t operate the business yourself, you still need to care about it. In our case, as investors helping build and scale a company, we also need an operator who knows how to execute the playbook successfully.

What lesson have you learned while building the company?Gordon: The lesson we’ve learned and continue to learn is that this business is all about people. People sometimes hear about The Little Gym and think it sounds simple. It’s a simple business to understand, but a challenging business to operate well.

The business depends on our ability to find the best people, train them effectively and give them career opportunities. That might mean moving beyond a teaching role, taking on more responsibility within a gym or eventually overseeing multiple locations.

What milestones are you working toward? Could revenue eventually reach $100 million?Condon: That sounds great.

Gordon: We’ve gone from seven locations to more than 30 in three years. We’ve quadrupled our footprint. Doing that again over the next couple of years would be aggressive, but we’re big believers in the brand and our business, and we want to keep growing.

We'll be in your inbox every morning Monday-Saturday with all the day’s top business news, inspiring stories, best advice and exclusive reporting from Entrepreneur.

Software squeezes more data out of Webb Telescope for imaging exoplanets

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Software optimizes a physical model of the telescope to reduce noise.

With a huge and still-growing catalog of exoplanets, researchers are eager to take the next steps: trying to infer what conditions are like on them, either by imaging them directly or by capturing the light that has passed through their atmosphere. Both are substantial challenges, given the presence of stars that are far, far brighter than light that has come to us via the planet.

While telescopes can be fitted with devices called coronagraphs that block out much of a star’s light, there are limits to their effectiveness, caused by noise from subtle variations in the telescope’s configuration and pointing between observations. Now, researchers have developed a new software package that optimizes a physical model of the telescope in a way that allows us to better model these differences, producing low-noise imaging of exoplanets.

Central to this work is the concept of a point spread function. If you use a telescope to image a single point of light, it will not appear that way in the actual image. Instead, it will be slightly blurry and spread out, due to imperfections in the telescope hardware and the limits of our ability to keep it pointed accurately through the time it takes to image something. The mathematical description of how a telescope distorts a point of light is called its point spread function.

There are two ways to figure out what the point spread function is. One is to use the telescope to image a known target and use those observations to derive a mathematical description of the point spread function. This is effective at reducing noise but has its limits, as the behavior of the telescope hardware can change over time, and the point spread function may vary with the wavelengths being imaged.

The alternative is to try to model the hardware’s behavior and use that model to derive a point spread function. But modern telescopes are incredibly complicated instruments, with many, many factors feeding into their performance in ways that can be difficult to ascertain, even with the full engineering schematics. As a result, these models simply contain too many parameters that need to be tuned to make this route computationally tractable.

But anyone paying attention to the world of AI developments will recognize that we’ve become quite successful at tuning billions of parameters in neural networks, in part through a mathematical technique called gradient descent. And, because this is central to training large neural networks, people have put a lot of work into optimizing the performance of gradient descent on GPUs.

So a team of researchers has developed a process they’re calling FLIP (a non-acronym for Physics-based Learning for Imaging Planets) that uses a physical model of the hardware (in this case, the NIRCam on the James Webb Space Telescope) with 106 possible parameters, and then uses software to optimize all its parameters. The optimization starts with a simulated point spread function, then uses gradient descent to optimize its performance on data from an actual star. Run on a current GPU, this process only takes a bit more than half an hour.

The researchers then fine-tune the results on the actual scientific image they are analyzing. This last part of the process is critical, since some variables will differ between observations.

To test how this works, the researchers used observations of two stars with known exoplanets (HIP 65426 and 51 Eridani), along with a third (TYC 5899) that doesn’t have any known exoplanets but is known to vary a lot between observations. These are young stars, and two of them have massive planets that are still warm from their gravitational collapse, allowing them to have been imaged previously.

Using Webb data, the team used FLIP to boost the signal-to-noise ratio by a factor of 1.5 to 2, depending on the observations. At a wavelength that can be used to detect carbon dioxide, they were able to spot one of the planets in the Webb data for the first time. For TYC 5899, the approach boosted the sensitivity of the observations by a factor of 15.

The researchers point out that the applications go beyond getting a better view of known planets. By lowering the noise, it should also be possible to spot dimmer (and hence older or lower-mass) ones that had previously eluded detection. They also think they can modify FLIP to highlight where it’s making the most dramatic changes to the model, which can potentially tell us useful things about what’s going on with the telescope’s hardware.

Nature, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-11213-z (About DOIs).

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

TikTok just released a standalone Shop app, but it's not in America just yet

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It offers exclusive deals and other goodies.

TikTok just released a standalone TikTok Shop app for iOS and Android, which was first spotted by social media consultant Matt Navarra. The app is currently available in several regions throughout the world, including Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand and the Philippines. Shopping is a huge part of the TikTok experience, so this kind of expansion makes sense.

The app looks like a streamlined version of the pre-existing shopping platform. It offers personalized product recommendations and lets people shop via livestreams and short-form videos. There are also app-exclusive deals, including free shipping offers. It promises "secure payments for safe checkout."

🚨 TikTok has launched a standalone TikTok Shop app!

→ Discover products, deals and recommendations → Shop through LIVE streams and short videos → Get app-exclusive offers and check out securely → Track orders and contact support pic.twitter.com/OjgDceXeAE

— Matt Navarra (@MattNavarra) November 5, 2026

This definitely signals that TikTok is looking to compete more directly with other e-commerce apps. The big mystery here is when the company will launch the app in more regions. We also don't know if it'll even come to America.

Remember, the American TikTok is a different company than the global TikTok. Engadget has reached out to the company on its plans for a stateside launch and will update this post when we hear back.

The American version of the platform has been adding new features. It recently introduced the ability to leave voice comments on any post and is integrating in-app travel booking services.

November officially marks Native Heritage Month, and while it’s always a great time to support Indigenous art, there’s no time like the present to honor the unique culture, traditions, and beauty found within North America’s Indigenous communities.

After all, these days, Indigenous ingenuity and craftsmanship expresses itself across all sorts of fashion and beauty categories. Amongst the cool, modern pieces you can find on the market today? Consider a graphic cardigan sweater from Cree designer Justin Jacob Louis, which was recently worn by Hudson Williams. Or there’s a graphic wrap top from Tlingit, Haida, and Squamish designer Pamela Baker, and a refined pleated skirt from Anishinaabe designer Lesley Hampton to go with it.

Jewelry offerings include a striking bolo necklace from Jamie Okuma, the CFDA’s only Indigenous designer, or shapely earrings from emerging labels like Copper Canoe Woman and Sacrd Thndr. Beauty lovers, meanwhile, can find a variety of products—from lipsticks to bronzers and highlighters—from Native brands such as Cheekbone Beauty or Prados Beauty. Whatever’s on your wish list, there is an Indigenous label out there to support—and it’s also a great time to start shopping for the holidays. So, what are you waiting for?
